Bodies are exactly capped at 262144 bytes (262145 refused). System.Text.Json escapes non-ASCII as `\uXXXX`, so signed bodies are pure ASCII. | Review 2a items 5, 6, 7. |
| M1-21 | 2026-11-04 | `FakePortal` reads `RequestUri.PathAndQuery`; the real portal reads Kestrel's `GetEncodedPathAndQuery`. A Kestrel-hosted interop test (real socket `HttpClient`, a verifier that mirrors the portal's) covers escaped JSON bodies, escaped query values (`%3A`, `%2F`, `%26`) and retries with fresh nonces, so the two readings are proven equal for what we send. | Review 2a item 8. |
| M1-22 | 2026-11-04 | **Receiver** (`POST /api/portal/webhooks`, anonymous). Order: 256 KB cap (262144 bytes; declared length and while reading) -> signature `v1=HMAC(Portal:WebhookSecret, "<ts>.<raw body>")` over the raw bytes in constant time with a 300 s window -> parse envelope -> process. Nothing is parsed before the signature passes. The webhook secret is its own key: the portal inbound secret and the web forwarding key both fail. **The signature does not cover `X-Solvelt-Event-Id`/`-Type`**, so the idempotency key is the signed body `id`; a header that differs is `400 event_id_mismatch` (otherwise a captured request replayed with a fresh header id would bypass the duplicate guard). 503 when the secret is unset. No rate limit on this route: it has one caller and a wrong signature costs one HMAC. Payloads are never logged (only the event type and outcome). | CONTRACT.md §1.2. |
| M1-23 | 2026-11-04 | **Idempotency and atomicity.** The event-id insert (`portal_inbound_events`, primary key = event id) is the first statement of the transaction; the handler's read-model changes commit in the same transaction. A unique violation means duplicate (`200`, nothing done); any other failure rolls everything back and answers `5xx`, so the portal's retry sees a clean slate. Two events racing to create the same projection state row conflict on its key or version: the loser rolls back with `5xx` and the portal retries (accepted; tested with the portal's retry loop). Tested with 24 parallel identical webhooks (exactly one application) on SQLite and PostgreSQL. | Brief: idempotent under parallel requests. |
| M1-24 | 2026-11-04 | **Projection framework.** One `IProjectionHandler` per event type (a duplicate registration fails at start-up). `ProjectionStore.TryAdvanceAsync(aggregate, key, fieldGroup, createdAt, terminalState?)` implements the contract: an older event never overwrites newer state (equal times apply), a terminal state is never reopened by a non-terminal event (also across field groups of the aggregate), a later terminal event may follow a terminal one (delivered then refunded). Handlers return Applied, Skipped or **Parked(aggregateType, key)**; parked events are stored with their payload and re-applied oldest-portal-time first, each in its own transaction, claimed by a row version so concurrent replayers apply one once (the claim only matters on PostgreSQL read committed; SQLite serialises writers; the test overlaps replayers with a barrier on PostgreSQL only). `ReplayParkedAsync` is called when an aggregate appears; an hourly worker (not in Testing) sweeps all parked events and runs retention. `EventRefs` parses `data.order`/`data.booking` and returns nothing unless the marketplace id AND the portal id are both present (never match on tracking number or SKU). The unknown-type path is the generic store: stored and acknowledged. Catalog handlers arrive with the catalog slice; order/booking handlers with M3-M5. Known gap: a parked event whose aggregate never appears stays parked (visible by status); an operator view comes with the outbox admin endpoints. | CONTRACT.md §1.2 points 3-4. |
| M1-25 | 2026-11-04 | **Retention** (see the PII table): payloads dropped after 30 days, id rows after 90 days (the id table is the replay guard and must outlive the portal's retry horizon), parked events keep payload and row until applied; the types `order.tracking_updated` and `shipment.delivered` (live driver positions, proof URLs) keep their payload only 24 h even when parked, and a parked one then expires to `skipped` (CONTRACT.md §5). On SQLite every `DateTimeOffset` is stored as the binary value (all times are UTC) so it orders and compares; PostgreSQL keeps `timestamptz`. | PII and contract §5. |
| M1-26 | 2026-11-04 | Amends M1-03: the first entities now exist, but EF migrations are generated once, as a single `InitialCreate`, in the **last M1 slice**, together with a test that the migrations apply on PostgreSQL and match the model. Until then Production has no schema management. | Avoids regenerating migrations on every slice before the first release. |
| M1-27 | 2026-11-04 | Test practice learned in this slice: caps are asserted against literals, never the production constant (a constant-reading test cannot notice the constant changing; the client's body-cap test had that flaw and is fixed); boundary tests use equal-length ids so the +1 byte really is +1; a swallowed exception can hide a missing guard (the replayer test no longer catches). Every guard in the receiver, processor, store and retention was mutated by hand and fails a test, except: the early `Content-Length` check (equivalent: the capped read refuses the same bodies) and the version claim on SQLite (see M1-24; verified on a local PostgreSQL 16 and in CI). | Review hotspot: tests must fail without the code. |
| M1-28 | 2026-11-04 | **Forwarding window 60 s** (review 3 F4, orchestrator decision O-09; was 300 s). A captured forwarding header set can be replayed to frame a victim's address, so it is only valid +-60 s (boundaries tested at +-60 accepted, +-61 rejected); there is no nonce. Operational requirements, documented in `docs/API.md`: the web tier's clock must be in sync with the API, and **the API must be reachable only from the web tier, over TLS**. | Shrinks the replay window. |
| M1-29 | 2026-11-04 | **Log filters enforced in code** (review 3 F3; superseded in part by M1-45, which wraps the logger factory because filters alone can be loosened). .NET selects the rule with the most specific category prefix whatever its order, so a coarse `AddFilter` is beaten by e.g. a config entry `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Routing.Matching.DfaMatcher: Trace` (a test found exactly that: DfaMatcher logs the request path at Debug). `AddSafeLogFilters` post-configures `LoggerFilterOptions`: every rule under `Microsoft.AspNetCore`, `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore`, `System.Net.Http.HttpClient` is removed (config, environment, command line, code) and the enforced rules (Hosting.Diagnostics None, AspNetCore/EF/HttpClient Warning) are added last. `appsettings.json` is read from `AppContext.BaseDirectory`, so the app behaves the same from any working directory; `ContentRootPath` is **not** pinned (the review suggested it) because `WebApplicationFactory` sets it and the host throws on a conflicting change; with the filters in code the content root no longer matters for safety. Tests: everything set to Trace in config still logs nothing sensitive; the real `dotnet Solvelt.Marketplace.Api.dll` run from another directory with Trace in the environment logs no URL, path, email or token; the enforcement rule is unit tested. | Review F3. |
| M1-30 | 2026-11-04 | **Forwarding failures are visible** (review 3 F1). Headers present but invalid (reasons `bad_signature`, `stale`, `incomplete`, `malformed`, `not_configured`) are counted, exposed as `forwarding.invalid` on `/health`, and logged as a warning at most once a minute with the reason and total only. Requests with no forwarding headers are not failures. The resolution is computed once per request. | A key mismatch or clock skew otherwise silently collapses all users into one bucket. |
| M1-31 | 2026-11-04 | **`AttemptLimiter` bounds** (review 3 F5). Expired windows are pruned at most every 30 s (not per call); the table is capped (default 100000 keys) and beyond the cap the oldest tenth is evicted in one batch, so memory is bounded, the cost is amortised, and recently attacked keys stay tracked. Review F7 (an aggregate /48 limit besides /64) is **not done**: it needs a chained limiter and the /64 plus per-account limits already bound a single subscriber; revisit if abuse appears. | Review F5, F7. |
| M1-32 | 2026-11-04 | **Catalog read model** (tables `catalog_*`): sellers (display name and active flag only), brands, categories (tree), listings (product or service) with variants, images (portal image id + dimensions), attributes, FAQs. Variants carry price and availability; the listing keeps a denormalised price range (over the variants that can be bought now, over all when sold out), a compare-at price and an availability flag so list queries stay cheap. Slugs are generated once from the title (accents folded by a table: `string.Normalize` does nothing under invariant globalization), unique, and never change when the title does (SEO). Products are added to a cart by **variant id**, never SKU (no SKU is stored). Listing, variant and category ids are portal ids with `ValueGeneratedNever` (a test found that EF otherwise treats a new variant with a key set as an existing row and issues an UPDATE that affects 0 rows). | Brief: catalog read model. |
| M1-33 | 2026-11-04 | **Versioning and visibility** (CONTRACT.md §2). A listing is versioned by the portal's `version`: a snapshot is applied only if newer than the applied `Version`, so replays, overlapping runs and stale poll pages are harmless and arrival order is irrelevant. `KnownVersion` is the highest version seen anywhere (events included): `KnownVersion > Version` means a fetch is pending. An unlist event of version u sets `UnlistedVersion`; the listing is unlisted while `UnlistedVersion >= Version`, so it is hidden at once and stays hidden against any snapshot of version <= u, but a snapshot with a higher version (a relist) shows it again. Visible = status `Active` and not a stub and not unlisted; the seller's active flag is joined at query time, so one snapshot deactivating a seller hides all of that seller's listings immediately. Everything non-visible is a 404 (no hint that it exists). | Review hotspot: the visibility rule. |
| M1-34 | 2026-11-04 | **Events** `listing.upserted` / `listing.unlisted` (`{listingId, version}`): the handler runs inside the webhook transaction and **never calls the network** (a test makes the source throw on any call). An upsert only raises `KnownVersion`; the sync job fetches the snapshot. An event for a listing we have not seen creates a hidden **stub** (placeholder slug `~<id>`, unique), so a later stale poll page cannot show what an unlist hid. A malformed payload (including a string `version`: `TryGetInt64` throws on non-numbers, which would have been a 500 and endless portal retries) is acknowledged and stored as `skipped`. Ordering is by portal version, not event time, so the catalog does not use `ProjectionStore`. | CONTRACT.md §1.2 and §2. |
| M1-35 | 2026-11-04 | **Sync job** (`CatalogSyncService`; hourly-style worker every `Catalog:SyncIntervalSeconds`, 60, not in Testing): reference data (categories, brands) -> cursor poll in pages (`Catalog:PageSize` 100, `MaxPagesPerRun` 50) -> fetch of listings with a pending version (`MaxPendingFetchesPerRun` 100). The page and its cursor commit in one transaction. A page that cannot be saved as a whole (one record breaks a database rule) is re-applied record by record and only the offender is skipped, so a single bad record never stalls the cursor; invalid snapshots (no variants, negative price, bad type or currency, no title, unknown category) are skipped and logged by id. A listing the portal answers "gone" for is marked `Removed` and hidden, and not asked for again. One instance runs at a time (a lease row, 5 minutes, taken over after expiry); a failure stores only the exception type (never a message, which may carry portal payload) and releases the lease; counts survive a failed run. | Brief: sync job, idempotent and order-independent by version. |
| M1-36 | 2026-11-04 | **Sources**: `ICatalogSource` is the only thing that knows the portal's feed; its DTOs are our provisional request C1 (CONTRACT.md §2), marked as such. `Catalog:Source` = `Fixture` (about 40 visible listings across 6 sellers, 10 brands, 13 categories, kosher food, Judaica and services, plus a suspended seller, an archived listing, sold-out and on-sale variants; ids derived from names so they are stable), `None` (empty catalog) or `Http` (not built yet: the portal's signed feed, a later slice). Default `Fixture` in Development/Testing and `None` elsewhere; **Production refuses `Fixture`** (validator). The fixture can be changed at run time (`Upsert`, `Add`, `Remove`) for tests and dev. | Brief: ICatalogSource with a fixture and a provisional HTTP source (the HTTP one follows C1). |
| M1-37 | 2026-11-04 | **Public endpoints** (anonymous, `public` rate limit): categories (tree, empty ones omitted, counts), brands (with visible listings), listings (filter by category incl. descendants, brand, seller, type; sorts newest, price_asc, price_desc, title; page and pageSize <= 100; ties break on id so paging is stable) and listing detail by slug or id (breadcrumb, variants, images, attributes, FAQs). Bad query values are `400 invalid_query`; ids in the query are parsed by us (the framework's own binding failure would be a different body). Title sorting uses an ASCII `TitleSort` key with a binary/`C` collation so SQLite and PostgreSQL order identically. Any `BadHttpRequestException` is now a 400 (it was a 500 outside the 413 case). Full shapes are in `docs/API.md`. Full-text search and SEO are M2. | Brief: public read endpoints. |
| M1-38 | 2026-11-04 | **Client deadline crash and disposal** (verification of 6ca0194). If a backoff timer fires after the total deadline, `remaining` is negative and `new CancellationTokenSource(remaining, time)` threw `ArgumentOutOfRangeException`. The loop now remembers the last retryable answer (or the last network failure) and, when no time is left at the start of an attempt, returns that answer (`Retryable`) or throws `PortalUnavailableException` with the cause; it never builds a timer from a non-positive delay. The response is disposed on every path (success, retry, oversized body, broken body, timeout). A `Retry-After` HTTP date in the past means "now" (a test with a non-zero backoff base would hang on an unclamped negative value). | A crash bug in code every workstream calls. |
| M1-39 | 2026-11-04 | **Tests replaced, not deleted (audit for the "no test deleted" rule).** In 6ca0194, `A_hung_portal_hits_the_per_attempt_deadline_and_is_retried` was replaced by `A_portal_that_never_answers_is_cut_off_at_the_total_deadline_on_the_injected_clock`, `A_configured_attempt_timeout_above_30s_is_clamped_to_30s`, `A_configured_total_deadline_above_60s_is_clamped_to_60s` and `The_last_attempt_is_bounded_by_what_is_left_of_the_total_deadline` (the old test made the responder throw the cancellation itself, so it tested nothing of the deadline), and the `429` case of `Retryable_answers_are_retried_for_ordinary_calls` was replaced by `A_429_is_surfaced_for_the_outbox_and_never_retried_by_the_client`, both because of orchestrator decision O-08 and M1-09 (429 is no longer retried by the client). The old body-cap tests that read the production constant were rewritten to assert the literal 262144 (M1-27). | Keeps the rule auditable. |
| M1-40 | 2026-11-04 | **Terminal states are absorbing unless a table allows the step** (orchestrator decision O-10, webhook review). The terminal state of an aggregate lives on ONE row per aggregate (field group `$terminal`, reserved), so it governs every field group, including ones that already have a row (the first version only looked across groups when creating a new row). A non-terminal event never reopens it. A terminal event must be a step in `TerminalStateRules` (the same state again is fine: repeated partial refunds). Orders: delivered to completed or refunded, completed to refunded (goodwill); **cancelled and refunded are absorbing**; an aggregate type with no rules is absorbing; other modules add steps with `Allow`. A terminal event older than the current terminal state is ignored. State names are `cancelled`, `refunded`, `delivered`, `completed` (`TerminalStates`), passed through `ProjectionContext.AdvanceAsync`. | Review of 65862ac, items 1 and 7. |
| M1-41 | 2026-11-04 | **Ordering is a total order at stored precision.** Times are compared at PostgreSQL's microsecond precision (so a value compares the same before and after it is stored) and ties are broken by event id (ordinal), kept in `projection_states.last_event_id`: the final state no longer depends on arrival order, and two events inside one microsecond cannot regress an aggregate. **Future-dated events are clamped:** the ordering time is `min(createdAt, receivedAt + 5 minutes)` (`portal_inbound_events.ordering_at`; the raw `createdAt` is kept; the clamp is logged without payload values; dates in the past are never changed; a parked event keeps the ordering time it got on receipt). Inside the five minutes a real event is still older than the clamped one and is skipped; after that it applies. `CommitAsync` uses `CancellationToken.None` so a client disconnect cannot leave an ambiguous commit. | Review of 65862ac, items 2, 6, 8. |
| M1-42 | 2026-11-04 | **Parked replay is claim-first and fair.** A replay claims the row with an atomic `UPDATE ... WHERE status = parked AND version = v` before it applies anything; 0 rows means another replayer has it. A database conflict inside the handler's own writes (on PostgreSQL two replayers creating the same first `projection_states` row get a unique violation, which used to escape and abandon the rest of the aggregate's list) rolls the claim back and counts as a lost claim: the event stays parked for the next pass. The sweep takes up to 200 aggregates per call ordered by `last_replay_at` (never tried first, then the longest ago, then the oldest event), and `ReplayParkedAsync` stamps what it leaves parked, so events that never resolve cannot starve the ones that can (the first version always took the 200 oldest events). | Review of 65862ac, items 3 and 4. |
| M1-43 | 2026-11-04 | **Parked events expire.** A parked event whose aggregate never appears keeps its payload for at most `Portal:InboundParkedMaxAgeDays` (30): it then becomes `skipped` with a null payload and the note "expired before its aggregate appeared" (the id row stays until day 90). The maintenance job warns, with counts only, about parked events older than `Portal:InboundParkedWarnAfterDays` (7). Tracking and delivery events keep their 24 h limit (M1-25). The PII table is updated. | Review of 65862ac, item 5 (PII). |
| M1-44 | 2026-11-04 | **Matching rule as code.** `EventRefs.Matches(orderRef, storedMarketplaceOrderId, storedSellerOrderId)` (and the booking twin) require the marketplace id AND the portal id to be **equal** to the stored ones, not merely present: every order and booking handler (M3-M5) must use it before changing a record, so an event naming our marketplace order id but another seller's order id cannot alter it. The webhook body cap is also tested for chunked requests without a Content-Length (only the in-read cap can catch those). | Review of 65862ac, item 9; M1 brief hotspot. |
| M1-45 | 2026-11-04 | **No logging configuration can re-enable URL logging** (verification of 207fabf; supersedes the filter-only part of M1-29). Three bypasses were found with real processes: a provider-specific rule (`Logging__Console__LogLevel__Default=Trace`) beats provider-less rules, a wildcard rule (`*Routing.Matching.DfaMatcher`) does not start with a sensitive prefix and its longer name wins, and removal was case sensitive while category matching is not. Filters are the wrong layer, so the guarantee is enforced where loggers are created: `ILoggerFactory` is wrapped (`SafeLoggerFactory`) and, whatever the filters, configuration, environment or command line say, loggers for `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Hosting.Diagnostics` and `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Routing*` are no-ops (no log line, no scope) and loggers for `Microsoft.AspNetCore*`, `Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore*` and `System.Net.Http.HttpClient*` are warning-and-above only with no scopes; matching is case-insensitive like .NET's. The filter post-configuration stays as a second layer and now also removes wildcard and differently-cased rules. Tests: the real `dotnet Solvelt.Marketplace.Api.dll` from another directory with each of the three env vars (and the original), in-process equivalents against the captured logs, and unit tests of the wrapper. | Defence in depth against a debugging mistake in production. |
| M1-46 | 2026-11-04 | **`AttemptLimiter` eviction and counting** (verification of 207fabf, F5). Eviction removes the lowest hit counts first, then the oldest window, so a spray of more than the table holds in single-hit keys cannot evict a key that is being brute-forced (the first version evicted the oldest window, which is the attacked key). The key count is an `Interlocked` counter (`ConcurrentDictionary.Count` takes every lock on every call); updates use a compare-and-swap loop so no hit is lost. A concurrency test found a real crash that was also in the previous version: LINQ over a `ConcurrentDictionary` copies through `ICollection.CopyTo`, which throws `ArgumentException` when another thread adds a key meanwhile; eviction now snapshots by enumerating. Tests: 16 threads started together on one key (exactly the limit allowed, no lost count), a spray with 16 threads (no exception, counter equals the table, bounded), an attacked-first key surviving a spray. | Review follow-up. |
| M2-01 | 2026-11-04 | **Search index** (`ISearchIndex`, table `catalog_search`). One row per listing holds its words in lower-case ASCII (the same `Slug.Words` folding is applied to queries, so both sides agree): title, brand, seller `searchTerms`, description. It is written in the same unit of work that applies a snapshot (so index and read model cannot disagree), a brand rename updates its rows, and every sync ends with an idempotent **backfill** of listings that have no row (a database from before the index, or a crash). **Visibility is not stored in the index**: every implementation joins the listing's `Visible` flag and the seller's `Active` flag at query time, so an unlist event hides a listing from search at once, and hidden rows can never use up the match cap (tested at the index level, since the endpoint filters visibility a second time and would hide an index leak). PostgreSQL: a **generated** weighted `tsvector` column (title A, brand B, terms C, description D) with a GIN index, prefix queries (`term:*`), rank = `ts_rank` of the prefix query + `ts_rank` of the exact query (so "ladle" beats "ladleful"), and when nothing matches a **`pg_trgm` `word_similarity` >= 0.5 pass** (every term, words of 4+ letters only) with a GIN trigram index on the combined text. The raw SQL uses parameters only; query terms are `[a-z0-9]+` after normalisation, so they cannot carry tsquery operators. SQLite (tests/dev): LIKE word-prefix match, scoring in code with the same weights, and an edit-distance pass (1 slip for 4-7 letters, 2 for 8+), at most 2000 rows scored. Both are covered by the same tests on both providers. | Brief: tsvector + GIN, weighted, prefix, typo tolerance via pg_trgm, SQLite fallback behind `ISearchIndex`, kept in sync by the catalog projection. |
| M2-02 | 2026-11-04 | **Search schema is created with `SearchSchema.Ensure`** after `EnsureCreated` (Development/Testing); `SearchSchema.PostgresDdl` is the idempotent script. Production has no schema management until M1's `InitialCreate` (M1-26): **that migration must run `SearchSchema.PostgresDdl` with `migrationBuilder.Sql`** and the database owner must be allowed `CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm` (or create it once by hand). EF cannot model the generated column. | EF Core has no model for a generated tsvector column. |
| M2-03 | 2026-11-04 | **Limits** (amended by M2-13: the 1000-match cap became a 50 000 safety bound). `q` at most 100 characters (101 is `400 invalid_query`), at most 8 words of 40 letters (the rest is ignored, not an error), at most 1000 ranked matches (so a search's `total` is at most 1000), suggest needs 2 characters and `limit` 1..10. Relevance order is computed in memory over those at most 1000 ids and only the requested page is loaded. A blank `q` is no search; punctuation only is a search with no terms and finds nothing. `sort=relevance` without `q` falls back to `newest` (the web's search page can send it unconditionally). Search text is never logged (M1-05 logs no query strings; a test pins it). At more than about 50 000 listings switch the typo pass to the indexed `<%` operator with a transaction-local `pg_trgm.word_similarity_threshold`. | Abuse bounds and privacy. |
| M2-04 | 2026-11-04 | **Provisional contract addition for C1** (to relay to the portal orchestrator): `ListingSnapshot.searchTerms` (optional array of seller keywords, each a short phrase; the portal's product search terms / tags). The field is optional and absent means no terms, so the read model works with a feed that does not send it. | Brief: weighted "search terms". |
| M1-47 | 2026-11-04 | **Transactional outbox** (`outbox_messages`). Producers call `OutboxWriter.Enqueue(type, payload, dedupeKey, delay)`, which only adds the row: the caller's own `SaveChanges` commits it together with the change that causes it (or rolls both back). A dedupe key is unique, so one payment event or one mail is queued once. The dispatcher selects due rows oldest first, **claims** each with an atomic `UPDATE ... WHERE id AND status = pending AND version = v AND lease free` (setting a 120 s lease and bumping the version), runs the handler **outside any transaction** (it calls the network), then finishes with a second compare-and-set on the claimed version. So several dispatchers (or API instances) never send one message twice, a dispatcher that dies leaves a lease that simply expires, and a result that arrives after the lease was lost is dropped because the newer claim's result stands (delivery is still at least once: handlers must be idempotent, and the portal dedupes on our event id). Failure `n` waits `30 s x 2^(n-1)`, capped at 3600 s (30, 60, 120, ... 1920); a handler's `Retry-After` can only lengthen that wait (also capped); the 8th failure dead-letters the message, and a handler can dead-letter at once (a refusal that waiting cannot change). Stored errors are short codes (an exception's type name, never its message, which may carry an address or token). Not in Testing, a hosted worker polls every 5 s and drains full batches. Tests: delivery, not-due, the exact schedule and the dead letter at 8, the cap, retry-after rules, ordering, six dispatchers over 12 messages (exactly once each, SQLite and PostgreSQL), lease take-over and the late-result case, dedupe, rollback with the caller's unit. The claim's version check and its lease check each suffice alone (removing one is not observable, removing both is caught), so both are kept as two guards. 40 mutations verified. | CONTRACT.md §6 ("at least once with backoff"), brief 2b-ii. |
| M1-48 | 2026-11-04 | **Handlers and portal answers.** One `IOutboxHandler` per type (a duplicate fails at start-up; an unknown type is retried as `no_handler`, never lost). `email.send` calls `IEmailSender`; `portal.payment-event` sends `POST /payment-events` with our own `eventId` (dedupe key `payment:<eventId>`). `PortalOutcome.From` maps any portal answer for every future handler: 2xx delivered; **`401` and `413` dead-lettered at once** (CONTRACT §1.1: never retry them; a 401 is a wrong key an operator must fix and then retry from the admin endpoint, a 413 never gets smaller; corrected in review 6, the first version retried 401); **`429` retried, never sooner than 60 s** (the portal's limiter is a one-minute window); the contract's retryable answers (`Retryable`), `408` and every `5xx` retried later; every other `4xx` (`400`, `404`, `422` such as `exceeds_sale`) dead-lettered with `portal_<status>_<code>` for a person, since waiting cannot fix it; an unreachable portal or a missing configuration is retried. | CONTRACT.md §1.1, §6. |
| M1-49 | 2026-11-04 | **Operations endpoints** `GET /api/admin/outbox[?status=&limit=]` (counts, newest first, never a payload) and `POST /api/admin/outbox/{id}/retry` (a dead message that still has its payload goes back to pending with 0 attempts; the check is inside the UPDATE, so two clicks revive it once; anything else is `409`, an unknown id `404`). Authenticated by `X-Solvelt-Admin-Key` against `Admin:ApiKey`, compared in constant time (hashes of both) and rate limited like the auth endpoints. It is a key of its own: at least 32 characters, never a dev default outside Development/Testing, different from the portal, webhook and web keys. **When it is unset the endpoints answer `404`** (disabled), so production does not need one to start. Development/Testing use a fixed dev key. The staff area (M7, O-06) will sit on top of these. Hardened in review 6: see M1-58. | Brief: admin retry endpoint; O-06. |
| M1-50 | 2026-11-04 | **Email provider (corrected in review 6).** `IEmailSender` is the seam. Development/Testing register `DevEmailSink` (the last 200 messages in memory, so a test or developer can read a link) unless `Email:Provider=Smtp` is set. **Outside Development/Testing the application does not start without `Email:Provider=Smtp`** (with `Email:SmtpHost` and `Email:From`; optional `SmtpPort` 587, `SmtpUserName`, `SmtpPassword`, `SmtpUseTls` true): without a provider no buyer could verify an address or reset a password, and the first version let every mail run through its 8 attempts and die after about an hour while the API looked healthy. `SmtpEmailSender` uses the framework's SMTP client; the password is configuration only and never logged. Every send is cut off after `Email:SendTimeoutSeconds` (20) and a timeout is an ordinary retry; the start-up validator (all environments) requires `Outbox:LeaseSeconds` to be at least the portal total deadline and at least the email timeout plus 30 s, so a message is never taken over while still being sent. A send that fails is retried with backoff and stores only the exception type. Mail payloads: see the PII table. | Orchestrator review of af8d339, items 2 and 7. |
| M2-05 | 2026-11-04 | **Filters and facets** (`ListingQuery`): price (overlap of the listing's buyable range with `[priceMin, priceMax]`), availability, type, seller, brand, category subtree, and attribute filters (OR within a key, AND across keys; `kosher` and `delivery` are shortcuts). **Delivery options are an attribute**: key `delivery`, values `ships`, `local`, `at-home`, `online` (the fixture sets them; for the real feed this is another provisional C1 convention to relay: products `ships`/`local`, services `at-home`/`online`). Facets are opt-in (`facets=true`, about ten cheap grouped queries) and **disjunctive**; a test proves every facet count equals the total you get by choosing that value. Only the whitelisted attribute keys (kosher certification/status/passover, delivery) get value counts. Repeated parameters are refused (except `attr`) so a client cannot send two meanings. Rating facet and rating sort wait for M6. | Brief: faceted filtering with counts. |
| M2-06 | 2026-11-04 | **Stable cursors** (keyset). `nextCursor` is the opaque position `(sort, key, id)` of the last item; the next page is `WHERE (key, id) is after the cursor`, so inserts and removals between requests cannot shift or repeat items (page numbers still work and are unchanged). Fetching one extra row decides whether there is a next page. Relevance cursors use the in-memory `(score, id)` order. A cursor is client input: it must belong to the request's sort, its key must be a value of the right type and range (a forged one is `400`, never a 500: a test found the first version threw), and it cannot be combined with `page`. Ties on the sort value (equal price, title or creation time) break on id; tested with five listings equal in all three. `sort=best_selling` is the newest order until M3 records sales (placeholder in the brief). | Brief: pagination with stable cursors. |
| M2-07 | 2026-11-04 | **410 Gone** for a listing that was public and is not now (unlisted, `Archived`, `Removed`): by slug and by id, `{ "code": "gone" }`. Still **404, no hint** for: a suspended seller's listings (moderation state must not be announced), drafts and any other status (never public), stubs (we only heard an event), unknown ids. Each guard is tested alone, including a stub whose placeholder seller id matches a real seller row. (The old "known limit" that a listing archived before we ever saw it Active also answered 410 is closed by M2-16.) **Tests changed, not deleted:** `Hidden_and_unknown_listings_are_a_404` lost its `retired-spice-gift-set` case (now `A_listing_whose_status_left_Active_is_410_gone_by_slug_and_by_id`), `A_hidden_listings_id_is_a_404_too...` keeps only the suspended-seller id (the archived id moved to that new test), and `An_unlist_event_hides_the_listing_immediately...` now expects 410 for the unlisted listing's detail; all because the M2 brief requires 410 for unlisted listings. | Brief: 410 for unlisted listings. |
| M2-08 | 2026-11-04 | **Slugs never change, so no slug-history table.** The brief's "301 for renamed slugs" is met by M1-32 (a title change keeps the slug; tested) plus the id route: `/listings/{id}` returns the canonical slug and the web tier 301-redirects an id URL (or an old `~stub` placeholder) to `/p/{slug}`. If the portal ever lets a seller edit slugs, a history table is added then. | Avoids a table with no writer. |
| M2-09 | 2026-11-04 | **Storefront and sitemap data.** Brand storefront by slug or id (seller, count, category tree), seller storefront by id (brands, category tree; there is no seller slug in the read model, so seller URLs use the id), both `404` when nothing is visible. `GET /catalog/sitemap/{kind}` pages keys with last-modified for listings (database paged), categories (newest time in the subtree), brands and sellers (aggregated in memory over visible listings: two columns per listing, fine up to the tens of thousands; revisit with a materialised table beyond that), ordered by id so pages are stable. | Brief: sitemap index with paged child sitemaps and lastmod; brand and seller pages. |
| M1-51 | 2026-11-04 | **Buyer sessions** (`buyer_sessions`). A session token is 32 random bytes (base64url, 43 characters), shown once at sign-in; only its **SHA-256** is stored, so a database leak yields no usable session. Expiry is idle (sliding) **7 days** and absolute **30 days** (`Auth:SessionIdleDays`, `Auth:SessionAbsoluteDays`): each use moves the idle limit forward but never past the absolute one, and the update is written at most every 5 minutes per session to spare the database. Each session also records **`authenticated_at`**, the last time the buyer proved who they are (password or passkey) in it: sign-in sets it, ordinary use does not move it, and a sensitive change checks it (decision O-11). A session is valid only while not revoked, inside both limits, and its account is verified and not deleted. `POST /auth/sign-out` revokes this session, `sign-out-all` every session of this buyer (never another buyer's), and a password reset revokes all. The web server keeps the token in its own HttpOnly cookie and sends `Authorization: Bearer`; a missing, malformed, unknown or oversized token is `401 unauthorized`. Tests: hash only in the database, slide and expiry boundaries, the absolute cap, the 5-minute throttle, sign-out scope, deleted accounts. | Brief 1: opaque hashed revocable sessions for an SSR front end. |
| M1-52 | 2026-11-04 | **Sign-up that cannot enumerate or hijack.** `POST /auth/sign-up` answers `204` for a new address, an address still waiting for verification and an address that already has an account (the same empty body); validation errors (`422`) cannot reveal an account because they are about the submitted values only. What differs is the email: a new or unverified address gets a verification link, a verified one gets "you already have an account" with sign-in and reset links (once an hour at most). **(Superseded by M1-64 and O-16, which closed a takeover this first version left open.)** The first version let a second sign-up for an unverified address replace its name and password; an attacker could then have the owner verify an account whose password the attacker chose. Now a sign-up for an unverified address changes nothing and only re-sends verification, and verifying needs the account's password. Mail volume is bounded to 3 sign-up mails an hour per address (the caller still sees `204`). The email address is trimmed and lower-cased and unique. | OWASP account enumeration and pre-hijacking. |
| M1-53 | 2026-11-04 | **Sign-in, lockout and reset.** Wrong password and unknown address are the same `401 invalid_credentials` and spend the same time (a dummy hash is verified for unknown or locked accounts). `403 email_not_verified` is only said after the **correct** password. **(The hard lock of the first version is replaced by the progressive delay of M1-63, decision O-17: a lock answered 429 at the sixth try for a known address and at the eleventh for an unknown one, which told them apart, could be used to lock a victim out, and survived a password reset.)** Verification links last **24 h**, reset links **60 min**; both are single use (consumed by an atomic `UPDATE`, so two clicks or two requests use it once), and a newer link invalidates older ones of the same kind; a link of one kind never works for the other. Links are built as in M1-56. `forgot-password`, `resend-verification` and `sign-up` are silent about unknown addresses and send at most 3 mails an hour per address. A reset also marks the address verified (the link proved the mailbox) and forgets the address's sign-in delay. `forgot-password` mails any existing account, verified or not (M1-64). The password is 10 to 128 characters and not the email address (no composition rules; a breached-password list is an open item). | Brief 1; W1-21. |
| M1-54 | 2026-11-04 | **Password hashing: ASP.NET Core Identity `PasswordHasher` V3** (PBKDF2, HMAC-SHA512, 128-bit random salt, 256-bit key, self-describing format) at **210 000** iterations (`Auth:PasswordIterations`, the OWASP figure for HMAC-SHA512), chosen over Argon2id because it ships in the framework (no extra dependency or native library to audit); a stored hash keeps its own iteration count and is **upgraded at the next sign-in** when the configured count is higher. Tests use 1 000 iterations and a test covers the upgrade. | Brief offers either; fewer moving parts. |
| M1-55 | 2026-11-04 | **Relay identity.** Every account gets one `relay-<16 random base32 characters>@<Marketplace:RelayDomain>` address at sign-up (unique, never changed, never derived from the real email) and the portal and sellers see the buyer only as that address and a public name: first name plus the initial of the last name ("Jane Brown" is "Jane B.", a single name stays, nothing is "Buyer"). The real email, full name and phone stay in the marketplace (CONTRACT.md §8). `RelayIdentity.PublicName` and `NewRelayEmail` are what order ingest (M3) must use. | CONTRACT.md §3 and §8. |
| M1-56 | 2026-11-04 | **Account mail holds no secret and no address in the database** (orchestrator review of af8d339, item 1). A verify or reset link is `<token id>.<HMAC-SHA256(Auth:EmailLinkKey, "link\|purpose\|id")>`: the `email_tokens` row has only the id, purpose, account and times, and the `auth.mail` outbox payload is `{ kind, accountId, tokenId }`. The address and the working link are produced **when the mail is sent** (the handler reads the address from the account and computes the MAC), so a pending, dead or backed-up outbox row, or a copy of the database, cannot yield a link or an address; only the key can, and it lives in the environment. A mail whose link was replaced, used or expired while it waited, or whose account was deleted, is not sent and counts as delivered. The MAC is compared in constant time and is bound to the purpose. `Auth:EmailLinkKey` is a key of its own (at least 32 characters, never the dev default outside Development/Testing, different from every other key); the start-up validator enforces it. Tests: payload and tables contain neither the link nor the address, forged, foreign-key, wrong-purpose and truncated links are refused, dead links are not mailed. | Review item 1 (reset links in clear in the outbox). Mutation checks (70 on the auth code) are all caught except guards that duplicate another guard and are only caught when removed together (a link's purpose is in both the MAC and the claim; the absolute session limit is also the cap of the sliding window) and the 100-character bearer length cap, which cannot be observed from outside. |
| M3-01 | 2026-11-04 | **Order of work (orchestrator handoff):** API slices first (cart; shipping + tax + quote; payment + checkout; ingest; refunds and disputes), then web once M2's PDP merges. Signed-in checkout first behind interfaces the guest path plugs into (guest order access links come from M1). | Handoff. |
| M3-02 | 2026-11-04 | **Cart** (`carts`, `cart_lines`). One cart per buyer (unique `AccountId`) or per guest (unique SHA-256 of a 32-byte token returned once; a client never chooses a token). Lines hold ids and a quantity (1..20, at most 50 lines, one currency) plus the unit price the buyer last saw, used only to say "price changed", never for money. Price, name, availability and visibility are read from the catalog on **every** view; unavailable lines count for 0 and block checkout; the request has no price field and a test pins that extra price fields are ignored. Services are not cart items (M4); a hidden/suspended/unlisted variant is 404. Add and merge are one atomic `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (cart_id, variant_id) DO UPDATE` (portable SQLite/PostgreSQL), so parallel adds of one variant make one line and cannot lose or exceed the cap; the buyer cart is created under a unique index and a loser re-reads. **Merge is claim-first**: only the request whose `DELETE` of the guest cart removes a row merges it (a retry, a parallel merge or an unknown token is a no-op). Groups are per (seller, brand). Known gap, accepted: the 50-line and currency checks read before the insert, so a burst of parallel adds of different variants can overshoot 50 by the burst size; checkout re-validates. Sale prices: the read model's variant price is the feed's current price (the feed applies `sale.startsAt/endsAt`); when the real feed (C1) exposes sale windows the read model must apply them with our clock (open item for M2). | Brief: server-side cart, merge, re-validation, split per (seller, brand). |
| M3-03 | 2026-11-04 | **Checkout quote** (`POST /api/v1/checkout/quote`, `QuoteService`): one server-side function prices a checkout from the catalog (re-read), the portal's shipping settings and the tax calculator, and returns every amount in `long` cents; the order and the payment amount of the next slice are built by the same function, so what the buyer saw is what is charged. The request has **no money fields** (extra fields are not deserialised; a test sends forged prices, totals, tax and a time and gets the honest result). Selections are choices (speed or local window), validated against what the portal offered in this same call; an unoffered speed, a full or unknown window, or a speed combined with a window is `422`. Default selection is the cheapest carrier speed. Local delivery is asked of the portal only for groups whose every listing has `delivery` = `local`, with OUR group items subtotal; a failing eligibility call only hides local delivery, while failing shipping settings is `503` (nothing is guessed). The window is re-checked by the portal at ingest (slice 4). | Brief: shipping per group, local delivery per group, server-side amounts. |
| M3-04 | 2026-11-04 | **Tax policy** (`ITaxCalculator`, `SimpleTaxCalculator`, config `Tax:*`). US destinations only; one percentage per state (`Tax:StateRates`, decimal, defaults for NY 8.875, NJ 6.625, CA 7.25, FL 6, TX 6.25, PA 6, OH 5.75, IL 6.25; unknown state or non-US = 0); no live provider. Items are taxable unless their category's root is in `Tax:ExemptRootCategories` (default `food-drink`: unprepared food; prepared food and other state-specific rules are **not** modelled, owner to review before launch). Shipping is taxed in `Tax:ShippingTaxableStates` (default NY, TX, PA, IL) when the group has a taxable item. **Rounding:** per line (and per group for shipping), `amount x percent / 100` in exact `decimal`, rounded half up (away from zero) to the cent, never floating point; totals are exact sums of the rounded parts (so the tax of an order can differ by a cent from rounding the sum, by design: sellers see the same line tax the buyer paid). The calculator is registered behind an interface so a provider can replace it. Tested: half-up cases (0.5 -> 1, 88.75 -> 89), exemption, per-state, no tax abroad or for an unknown state, sum identities. | Brief: documented simple policy, per-line `taxCents`, rounding documented. |
| M3-05 | 2026-11-04 | **Shipping prices** follow CONTRACT.md §5 exactly: the portal's default profile, the zone listing the destination country, each enabled speed priced flat or `baseCents + ceil(weightGrams / 1000) x perKgCents`; free when the **items subtotal of that (seller, brand) group** reaches the profile's threshold (tested: a group below it pays although the cart is above it; exactly at it is free). A speed that cannot be priced is not offered. Settings are cached 5 minutes per (seller, brand) in a singleton, coalesced (eight parallel quotes make one portal call) and a failed answer is never cached (M1-11 handed this to M3). **Weight is a known gap:** the catalog read model has no per-variant weight, so grams come from the listing's `weight` attribute ("550 g", "1.5 kg", "12 oz", "2 lb"; rounded up) and an unknown weight counts as 0, which under-charges a weight-based speed. Request for the portal orchestrator (C7, via the orchestrator): an explicit `weightGrams` per variant in the catalog feed; until then sellers must set the `weight` attribute on weight-priced products. Estimates: handling days plus transit business days (standard 5, expedited 2, overnight 1, others 5; `Shipping:TransitDays`) from today on our clock, to the earliest date plus 1 (fast) or 2 (3+ days) business days; no holidays. | Brief: speeds and prices per CONTRACT.md §5, handling days to delivery estimate. |
| M3-06 | 2026-11-04 | **Orders and the double-submit rule.** `marketplace_orders` (+ groups per seller/brand, lines) are built from the quote only (M3-03), in `long` cents; the order total equals the sum of its groups equals the intent amount (tested on the stored rows). **One pending order per identical checkout** is enforced by a unique `ActiveKey` = SHA-256 of everything agreed (account, cart, server prices and tax, deliveries, address, gift note); it is cleared when the order leaves `pending_payment`, so the same cart can be bought again later. A parallel duplicate loses the insert race, re-reads the winner and both continue with the same order. **One PaymentIntent**: the intent is created with Stripe's `Idempotency-Key` = `pi:<order number>` and stored with `UPDATE ... WHERE PaymentIntentId IS NULL`, so the 12 parallel requests of the test reach Stripe with one key and the fake (which implements Stripe's key semantics) holds one intent; a stored/returned mismatch is logged and answered 503. The client secret is not stored: a repeat call returns it through the idempotent create. A changed checkout makes a new order; older pending orders of the cart are superseded and their intents cancelled best effort (a payment that still lands on a superseded order is refunded in full, never fulfilled: M3-18, which replaces the earlier "honour it" rule). Pending orders older than 23 h (inside Stripe's 24 h key lifetime) are expired by the worker in slice 4. Order numbers are 8 random characters from a no-lookalike alphabet and a unique index; a collision retries with a new number. Mutation checks: a random ActiveKey, or an idempotency key from the unsaved order's number, fails the parallel test. | Handoff: parallel double-submit gives one order and one PaymentIntent. |

| M3-06 | 2026-11-04 | **Orders and the double-submit rule.** `marketplace_orders` (+ groups per seller/brand, lines) are built from the quote only (M3-03), in `long` cents; the order total equals the sum of its groups equals the intent amount (tested on the stored rows). **One pending order per identical checkout** is enforced by a unique `ActiveKey` = SHA-256 of everything agreed (account, cart, server prices and tax, deliveries, address, gift note); it is cleared when the order leaves `pending_payment`, so the same cart can be bought again later. A parallel duplicate loses the insert race, re-reads the winner and both continue with the same order. **One PaymentIntent**: the intent is created with Stripe's `Idempotency-Key` = `pi:<order number>` and stored with `UPDATE ... WHERE PaymentIntentId IS NULL`, so the 12 parallel requests of the test reach Stripe with one key and the fake (which implements Stripe's key semantics) holds one intent; a stored/returned mismatch is logged and answered 503. The client secret is not stored: a repeat call returns it through the idempotent create. A changed checkout makes a new order; older pending orders of the cart are superseded and their intents cancelled best effort (**a payment that still lands on a superseded order must be honoured by the payment webhook, slice 4: money was taken**).
