| M3-07 | 2026-11-04 | **Stripe seam.** `IPaymentProvider` (create, cancel, refund; every write needs OUR idempotency key). `StripeHttpProvider` talks to Stripe's REST API with form bodies and Bearer key (no SDK dependency: the header and request shape are ours and unit tested against a scripted handler; Stripe's message is never echoed because it can carry card details; 429, 409 and 5xx and network errors are "unavailable, retry with the same key", other 4xx are final `PaymentRejectedException` codes). `FakePaymentProvider` (Development/Testing without a key, and every CI test) implements Stripe's semantics: a repeated key returns the first result, a reused key with other parameters is refused, a refund can never exceed what was captured, minimum 50 cents. No key outside Development/Testing means `UnconfiguredPaymentProvider`: checkout answers 503 and nothing is charged (the application still starts). Keys only from env; `StripeSignature` (used by the webhook, slice 4) verifies `t=,v1=` over the raw bytes in constant time within 300 s and accepts several `v1` (rotation). | Brief: fake provider in CI, idempotency keys on every Stripe call. |
| M3-08 | 2026-11-04 | **Start-up rules for Stripe** (all environments): a secret key must start `sk_test_`/`rk_test_`, a publishable key `pk_test_`; anything containing `_live_` stops the application. Outside Development/Testing `Stripe:WebhookSecret` is required once a secret key is set, at least 32 characters, never the dev default, and different from every other key (portal inbound, portal webhook, web forwarding, admin, email link). Dev/Testing get a fixed dev webhook secret. | Handoff: keys only from env, test mode only, webhook secret distinct from every other key. |
| M3-09 | 2026-11-04 | **Order ingest** (`order.ingest` outbox handler, queued in the same transaction that marks the order paid). The body is built from stored ids and server amounts only: lines by `productId` + `variantId` (never SKU), `marketplaceOrderId` = our order number, the relay identity and public name, `shipping[]` per group, `deliveries[]` for local groups, `placedAt` = our payment time. Retries re-send the same `marketplaceOrderId` (the portal answers `201` or `200 created:false`, both stored the same way; a fresh nonce per attempt is the client's job). **Match rule:** every returned seller order must name a (seller, brand) equal to one of OUR groups, each group exactly once; anything else is dead-lettered `seller_order_mismatch` and never guessed. Storing the seller orders, the status change to `placed`, one `payment.captured` per seller order (event id `captured:<order>:<seller order number>`, amount = that group's total, `paymentRef` = the intent) and the buyer's confirmation mail are one transaction; a parallel delivery loses the status claim and does nothing. `422 invalid_order` (nothing created at the portal; includes `delivery_window_full` etc.) = order `failed`, full refund reserved, buyer mailed that they were not charged and why. Oversold seller orders are stored (`Oversold`) and will be shown. **Two contract gaps (request C8, accepted by the portal, interim mapping below, switch in M3-15):** (1) the portal takes ONE `shippingMethod`/`shippingSpeed` per order but our groups can choose different speeds: we send the FASTEST chosen speed (so no seller plans slower than promised); a per-group `speed` in `shipping[]` is requested. (2) the portal's total has no field for tax on shipping, so tax charged on shipping is **folded into the group's taxable lines' `taxCents`** (proportional to their tax, remainder on the first line; tested to lose no cent) and the portal's seller-order total equals what the buyer paid, which `payment.captured.amountCents` also states. | CONTRACT.md §3, §6; brief order flow. |
| M3-10 | 2026-11-04 | **Stripe webhook** (`POST /api/stripe/webhooks`): verified with `Stripe:WebhookSecret` only (the portal secrets, the admin key and a wrong key all fail), before any parsing; 256 KB cap while reading; event id inserted first in ONE transaction with the handler's changes (duplicate = 200, nothing done; failure = 5xx, nothing recorded). No payload is stored (card details), only id, type, Stripe time, outcome and the object id. Handlers read state, not arrival order: `payment_intent.succeeded` moves pending/superseded/expired to `paid` with one conditional UPDATE (a second event, in parallel or later, matches 0 rows), and `payment_failed` only writes a decline code to a still-pending order, so any order of the two ends in the right state. **Match rule:** the order is found by its number from the intent metadata and accepted only if its stored intent id is the event's (a webhook that beat the checkout request's own save adopts the intent id once); an event naming our order number but not yet saved is a 5xx. The amount and currency must equal the priced order; otherwise the order is failed (`amount_mismatch`) and `PaidCents` (what Stripe took) is refunded, never the priced total. Superseded and expired orders that get paid are honoured. | Handoff: Stripe webhook idempotent, order independent, own secret. |
| M3-11 | 2026-11-04 | **Refund cap** (`RefundService`, `order_refunds`). A refund is first RESERVED by conditional updates (`RefundedCents + amount <= PaidCents` on the order, `<= TotalCents` on the seller order) plus a unique key (`fail:<order>`, later `portal:<portal refund id>`) and the outbox message, in one transaction; the unique key stops a replayed webhook or event creating a second refund, and 8 parallel requests of 60 % of one seller order reserve exactly one (tested, with the exact boundary: the remainder fits, one cent more does not, one seller order's cap does not use the other's, the whole order cannot exceed what was paid). `stripe.refund` calls Stripe with `Idempotency-Key = refund:<key>`; success, the `refund.processed` event for the portal (portal-requested refunds only) and the status change commit together; a Stripe outage is retried with the same key; a refusal (for example more than was captured, after a refund in Stripe's dashboard) releases the reservation, marks it `rejected` and dead-letters the message for a person. Mutation checks: removing either cap condition, or the 422 handling, fails tests. | Handoff: refunds never exceed what was paid, per seller order. |
| M3-12 | 2026-11-04 | **Order maintenance** (worker every 10 min, not in Testing): a pending order older than 23 h (inside Stripe's 24 h idempotency lifetime) expires, its key is released and its intent cancelled best effort; a payment that still arrives is refunded in full (M3-18); never-paid expired or superseded orders are deleted after 30 days (90 when they have an intent). | Keeps one intent per checkout valid and the table small. |
| M3-13 | 2026-11-04 | **Portal-requested refunds** (`PortalRefundHandler` for `refund.issued` and `order.cancelled.refundDueCents`, in the webhook transaction, no network). Matching: our order number AND the portal's seller order id equal to a stored group (`EventRefs.Matches`); an unknown order number is skipped (not ours), a known order whose seller orders are not stored yet is **parked** and re-applied by `ReplayParkedAsync` right after the ingest stores them (tested). The refund key is `portal:<refundId>` (or `cancelled:<event id>`), unique, so a replayed event, the same refund under another event id, or an outbox retry makes one refund. **Tax:** the portal's ledger never sees tax (PAYMENTS.md §2: Net = items + shipping - fee; `refund.processed` carries items and shipping) but the buyer paid it, so the buyer gets back `items + shipping + tax`, tax in proportion to the net refunded (`tax x net / (items + shipping)`, round half up) and the LAST refund of a seller order returns exactly the remaining tax (tested: 89 + 328 = 417 on a 417 seller order; parallel refunds return it once). Stripe is refunded the total; `refund.processed` reports items and shipping only, so the portal's `exceeds_sale` cannot be triggered by tax. Cancellations are clamped to what is left (a portal that states `refundDueCents` including tax gets the same result); a full cancel returns the seller order's shipping too, a `partial` remainder cancel none. A refund the portal asks for above what is left is skipped, logged and never reserved (a bug alarm, not a retry loop). The reservation is a compare-and-swap on the group counters plus both caps (`RefundedCents + total <= TotalCents` on the group, `<= PaidCents` on the order); the CAS and the total cap each cover most races alone on SQLite, so both stay. **Open question for the portal (C8):** does `refund.issued.amountCents` already include tax? We clamp to items + shipping either way. | CONTRACT.md §3, §6; PAYMENTS.md §2, §6. |
| M3-14 | 2026-11-04 | **Stripe refund and dispute events.** `charge.refunded`: our refunds are reserved before Stripe is called, so Stripe's cumulative `amount_refunded` can never exceed what we reserved; when it does, someone refunded in the Stripe dashboard: it is recorded as an `external` refund against the cap (idempotent per total) and logged at error level for a person; the portal is not told (no seller order can be named). `charge.dispute.created|updated|closed|funds_withdrawn|funds_reinstated` become `dispute.created|updated|closed` per seller order of the payment (matched by our stored intent id), with our event ids (`dispute:<stripe event id>:<seller order number>`) through the outbox. The dispute amount is first reduced to the share that was seller money (items + shipping of all groups over the total paid), then split over the seller orders by what each sold (largest remainder, exact sum, tested); the portal's default dispute fee is left to apply once (the first group says nothing, the others say 0). Status mapping: `needs_response`, `under_review`, `won`, `lost`, `warning_closed` are sent; other inquiry states (`warning_needs_response`, `warning_under_review`) and `charge_refunded` are not (the portal receives Stripe's own `charge.dispute.*` for inquiries, PAYMENTS.md §7). A dispute on an order that failed is ignored; one on an order whose seller orders are not stored yet is a 5xx so Stripe retries. | PAYMENTS.md §6-7. |
| M2-10 | 2026-11-04 | **Reference data cannot stall the sync** (review B-1). Categories and brands from the feed are made safe before they touch the database: records without slug or name dropped, names cut to 200 and slugs to 120, one record per id; **slugs are globally unique** (they are URLs; the portal may only keep them unique per parent): a row keeps its current slug when the feed's is taken, a new row gets an id suffix (6, 12, then 32 hex characters), so a feed slug equal to a generated fallback-brand slug no longer collides; parent links become a forest: self-parents, unknown parents and cycles (cut at the edge that closes them, smallest id first) become roots, and a category deeper than **10 levels** is attached to its ancestor at level 9 (nothing is dropped). If the batch still cannot be saved, the records are applied one at a time and only the one that breaks a database rule is skipped (id logged, never the slug or the message). A failing reference source or a bad record no longer stops the poll: the error is recorded and the listings go on. **Test replaced:** `A_failure_in_reference_data_is_recorded_and_changes_no_listing` (it enshrined the stall) by `A_failure_in_reference_data_is_recorded_but_does_not_stop_the_listings` and `Listings_held_for_a_missing_category_appear_once_the_reference_data_works_again`. | Review B-1: one bad record made `applied = 0` forever. |
| M2-11 | 2026-11-04 | **A listing whose category is unknown is held, not dropped.** The snapshot used to be skipped while the cursor moved on, so a late category feed lost listings until their next change. It is now stored as a hidden stub that remembers the version it waits for (`KnownVersion > Version`), and the pending fetch applies it once the category exists. **Pending fetches back off** (review 6): each row has `FetchAttempts` and `NextFetchAt` (1, 2, 4 ... minutes, at most 6 hours, reset by a successful apply or by an event announcing a newer version); the run takes the rows that are due, never-tried first, so a hundred snapshots that never apply cannot starve good ones nor cost a hundred portal calls per run. **Test changed:** `An_invalid_snapshot_is_skipped_and_does_not_block_the_cursor` now expects the unknown-category listing as a hidden stub (the four truly invalid snapshots are still absent). | Review 6 and B-1. |
| M2-12 | 2026-11-04 | **A bad record and a bad moment are different** (review B-2). Only a **deterministic** failure skips a record (`SyncErrors.IsDeterministic`: PostgreSQL SQLSTATE classes 22, 23 and 54, SQLite constraint, or EF/logic refusing the record). Timeouts (EF wraps them as `InvalidOperationException`, which the old code skipped), dropped connections, deadlocks, cancelled statements and concurrent updates are **transient**: the page rolls back, the cursor stays, the run fails, and the worker waits longer after each failure (the interval doubled, at most 10 minutes). The snapshot read uses `AsSplitQuery` (200 variants x 30 images x 20 attributes x 10 FAQs took 27 s as one query); in Testing EF **throws** on an unsplit multi-collection Include, so no test can regress it. Limits per snapshot: **200 variants** (more is an invalid snapshot, skipped: variants are never cut because a buyer must be able to buy each), 50 images, 100 attributes, 50 FAQs (the rest is cut); text is cut to its column (title 300, summary 600, names and alt 300, attribute key 100 and value 500, question 500, seller and brand name 200) instead of dropping the listing (review 9). **Logging:** EF's own error logs (`Microsoft.EntityFrameworkCore.Update` and `.Database.Command`) carry the database exception whose message can quote row values, so they are now silent categories like hosting diagnostics (M1-13/M1-17 extended; three M1 logging test cases moved from "warning only" to "silent"). **Found on the way:** `ProblemExceptionMiddleware.Write` calls `Response.Clear()`, which dropped the `Retry-After` header the rate limiter set just before it, contrary to docs/API.md; headers are now set after the clear and a test pins `Retry-After: 60` on a 429. | Review B-2, 9 and an M1 logging hotspot. |
| M2-13 | 2026-11-04 | **Search: filters before the cap, indexed typo pass, Unicode** (orchestrator reviews of 445ed53 and decision O-13). (1) The 1000-match cap applied before filters, so `q=widget&type=service` found nothing among 1100 widgets; matches are now bounded at **50 000** (a safety bound: filters, facets and every sort apply to all of them) and a test with 1100 strong and 5 weak matches pins totals, the service filter, the cheapest-first sort, facets and a cursor walk. Revisit with a subquery join if one query can match more. (2) The PostgreSQL typo pass uses the **`<%` operator** (the GIN trigram index serves only the operator, the function form scanned every row) with `pg_trgm.word_similarity_threshold` set by `set_config(..., true)` inside the search's own transaction (so it never leaks to a pooled connection); the plan is checked for the operator form on the indexed column. (3) **Own tokenizer** (`SearchText`): Unicode letters and digits, lower-cased; combining marks dropped without splitting the word (Hebrew niqqud and cantillation, Arabic harakat, decomposed Latin accents); maqaf, paseq and sof pasuq are separators, geresh, gershayim and apostrophes are not; Latin accents folded as in slugs; full-width ASCII, Latin ligatures and Hebrew presentation forms folded by hand because `string.Normalize` does nothing under invariant globalization (checked); words cut at 64 characters on whole characters (PostgreSQL refuses a lexeme above 2046 bytes; SQLSTATE 54 is deterministic so one such word cannot stall the sync). URL slugs stay ASCII. CJK has no word segmentation: a run is one word, matched by its beginning. (4) The PostgreSQL vector is built from OUR tokens by the immutable function `catalog_tokens_tsvector` (one lexeme per token **with its position**, because a weight lives on a position; `array_to_tsvector` stores none and made every field rank alike, found by the ranking tests) instead of `to_tsvector`, so the database locale cannot change what a word is; queries are quoted lexemes cast to `tsquery`, not parsed. The column carries a version comment and is rebuilt once when the definition changes. **Typo tolerance for non-Latin text needs a UTF-8 database locale** (pg_trgm); exact and prefix search do not. (5) SQLite typo pass: a row with no words no longer throws (it was a 500); it is edit distance, PostgreSQL's is trigram similarity, so borderline typos differ (exact and prefix agree). (6) Tie-breaks by id are pinned with 20 equal listings inserted in descending id order, for every sort, by cursor and in suggestions (EF inserts in key order, so physical order hides a missing tie-break: rewritten rows move to the end of the table in the suggest test). | Reviews of 445ed53, O-13. |
| M2-14 | 2026-11-04 | **Categories per request.** `CategoryMap` (scoped) reads the category table once per request and builds the parent to children lookup once: the filter, the facets, the cards, the breadcrumb and the suggestions share it (a test counts the reads), and "this category and everything below it" is one visit per node (it was a scan of every category per step: 3000 deep took 5.8 s). The tree endpoint stops at depth 12 whatever is stored (System.Text.Json's depth limit made `/categories` answer 500 from about 33 levels), the breadcrumb is bounded and ends at a repeat. No cross-request cache: the table is small and an indexed read per request is cheaper than invalidation rules. | Review 3. |
| M2-15 | 2026-11-04 | **Operations.** The sync lease has an **owner token**: only the owner renews (per page and per pending fetch) or releases it, and a run that finds its lease taken over stops and writes nothing (two tests, one with a clock past expiry). Seller state (display name, active flag) is **ordered by the portal time of the snapshot that carried it** (compared and stored at millisecond precision: SQLite keeps a binary long with millisecond resolution, PostgreSQL microseconds, so only a coarse common precision makes a tie mean the same; a tie applies), so an older page cannot reactivate a suspended seller; the fixture's `Upsert` now stamps a newer `UpdatedAt`, as a portal edit does. **Seller retention (PII):** a seller's display name is deleted 90 days (`Catalog:SellerRetentionDays`) after the last time the seller was active with a visible listing; boundaries tested at 89d 23:59:59, 90d and 90d 1s, and counted from the last visible time, not from first sight. Catalog responses carry `Cache-Control: public, max-age=5` on 200 and `no-store` otherwise (review 8). `Catalog:Source=Http` is **refused by the start-up validator** until the feed exists (it passed validation and threw at first use; review 10). Portal C5 note (orchestrator): when the explicit derived `delivery` field lands, the delivery reader prefers it and falls back to the `delivery` attribute; not built yet. | Reviews 5, 7, 8, 10, 11. |
| M2-16 | 2026-11-04 | **Review-2 of facets and SEO.** **410 needs a past**: `WasPublic` is set the first time a listing becomes visible and never cleared; `IsGone` requires it, so a draft plus a `listing.unlisted` event, or a listing first seen archived, is 404 and never confirms a listing that was not public (amends M2-07: the seed's archived listing is now 404; the tests that used it as a 410 example now archive a public listing first). **Cursors** hold `(sort, key, id, fingerprint)`: the fingerprint is a short hash of everything that decides which listings come back and in what order (effective sort, type, seller, brand, category, price range, availability, attributes, search words; not page size or `facets`), so a cursor continues only its own request (`400` otherwise); the sort is checked against the order that will actually RUN (relevance without `q` is the newest order, so a relevance cursor with a cleared search box is refused instead of parsing a score as a date); the key must have the shape we ever wrote (title keys are slugs, numbers are digits, nothing with a control character), and JSON `null` parts are refused (one was a 500). **Control characters** (NUL is a PostgreSQL 22021 error, not a 400) are refused with 400 in every parameter except `q` (it is normalised to letters and digits) and in cursors; in route values they are 404 (the host itself refuses a NUL in a path). Documented limit: keyset paging can repeat or skip a listing whose own sort value changes between two pages. **Sitemaps** carry `ETag` (hash of the page), `Last-Modified` (newest entry, to the second) and answer 304 (`If-None-Match`, else `If-Modified-Since`; a 304 is cacheable like the 200); categories and brands have `UpdatedAt` (set by the sync when created or changed) as a floor for last-modified, and a category also carries the change times of the categories below it, so none moves backwards when its newest listing leaves. A brand storefront shows a seller that really has its visible listings (the brand feed's seller when it does, else the seller with most) and no longer throws when the brand row names a missing seller; the sellers facet is capped at 500 like brands. Facets: the price facet's own-filter exclusion is now tested on both ends; the availability facet's exclusion cannot be told apart by any test because counting available listings gives the same number with or without the `available` filter (an equivalent mutant, kept for symmetry). | Orchestrator review of 65e4577 + 3768a5c. |
| M5-01 | 2026-11-04 | **Fulfilment projection** (`Fulfilment/FulfilmentProjector`, tables `seller_order_states`, `order_shipments`, `order_shipment_lines`, `order_line_fulfilment`, `order_cancel_requests`, `order_returns`, `order_return_lines`). One state row per seller order (group). Ordering/terminal rules per SELLER order (aggregate key `MP-…/<sellerOrderId>`): status steps go through `AdvanceAsync`; shipment facts (a parcel exists, was delivered, was voided) and line counters are monotonic/additive and apply even when the status step is skipped; shipped units are recomputed from the non-voided parcels (never incremented), attempt failures store the portal's attempt number (never a counter), so replays cannot inflate anything. Matching is `EventRefs.Matches` (our id AND the portal id); return and cancel events are additionally matched on OUR `returnId`/`requestId` AND the seller order. Only absolute https URLs are kept (tracking, label). | Brief; review hotspots (matching, order independence). |
| M5-02 | 2026-11-04 | **One handler per event type** (registry rule) means `order.cancelled` and `refund.issued` stay M3's `PortalRefundHandler`; it now runs the money part first and, unless that parked, calls `FulfilmentProjector.ApplyAsync` (so the projection runs exactly once and the event outcome is still M3's). All other types are `FulfilmentHandler`. `refund.issued` ends the seller order as `refunded` (terminal) only when `full: true`; a partial refund adds refunded units and the order continues. A partial `order.cancelled` (remainder) adds cancelled units and does not end the order. The money is never touched here. | Handoff: don't register a second handler; don't move money. |
| M5-03 | 2026-11-04 | **Buyer actions are stored before the portal call** and sent from the outbox (`portal.cancel-request`, `portal.return-request`, `portal.delivery-confirmation`; dedupe keys carry our ids). Cancel: our `requestId` `CR-…`; at most one OPEN request per seller order (unique `OpenKey`), so parallel clicks are one request. Return: our `returnId` `RT-…`, optional `Idempotency-Key` header (unique per account). Quantity cap: `shipped − returned − refunded − pendingReturn` is enforced by one conditional UPDATE per line inside the transaction that stores the return (parallel requests cannot take the same unit; a rejected/failed return releases its units; a received one turns them into returned units; each line is released once). We follow the portal's formula literally, so a line that was returned AND refunded counts twice (conservative; the portal re-validates). Delivery confirmation: allowed only when the whole seller order is `shipped`; claimed by one conditional UPDATE of `BuyerConfirmedAt` = OUR clock; the portal body has no `deliveredAt` and the endpoint DTO has no time field (a client value is never deserialised); a refusal by the portal clears the claim so the buyer can retry. Another buyer's order is a 404 on every route. | Brief; review hotspots (client time, idempotency, quantity cap). |
| M5-04 | 2026-11-04 | **Return label links.** The link from `return.label_created` (or a refresh through `POST …/returns/{id}/label-link`, a synchronous call within the client's 30 s deadline) is stored only if it is https and not yet expired, is shown only while alive, and is nulled by the maintenance job at expiry. | Contract §5; PII. |
| M5-05 | 2026-11-04 | **Retention of order PII** (`FulfilmentMaintenance`, run by the order worker): 90 days after EVERY seller order of an order is delivered, cancelled or refunded, the delivery address, coordinates, phone, gift message, buyer name and relay address on `marketplace_orders` become `Redacted`/null, and the buyer's cancel notes and return comments are nulled. Money, lines and ids stay. (M3 left the address retention to M5.) | M3 hand-off; ORDERS.md §7. |
| M1-57 | 2026-11-04 | **Tests replaced in review 6 (audit for the "no test deleted" rule).** `Without_an_email_provider_mail_waits_in_the_outbox` (the unconfigured placeholder sender no longer exists) became `A_failing_email_provider_leaves_the_mail_pending_and_stores_only_the_exception_type` and `An_email_send_that_never_answers_is_cut_off_inside_the_lease_and_retried`, with `Production_refuses_to_start_without_an_email_provider` covering the missing provider; `Mail_without_a_provider_waits_and_keeps_no_secret_while_it_waits` (auth) became `Mail_that_cannot_be_sent_waits_in_the_outbox_and_keeps_no_secret_while_it_waits` (same checks, a failing sender instead of the placeholder); the `401` case of `A_payment_event_the_portal_cannot_take_now_is_retried_later` and the `401` row of `Portal_answers_map_to_outbox_outcomes` (which pinned "401 is retried") now expect a dead letter, because CONTRACT §1.1 says never retry 401 (new tests `A_payment_event_the_portal_refuses_with_401_or_413_is_dead_lettered_at_once_and_counted`, `A_429_is_not_tried_again_sooner_than_the_portals_one_minute_window`); `Admin_endpoints_need_the_key` (expected `401`) became `Admin_endpoints_answer_404_to_a_missing_or_wrong_key`, now also with a prefix, the key plus one character and another case. The production-configuration helpers of the other test files gained the email settings. | Keeps the rule auditable. |
| M1-58 | 2026-11-04 | **Operations endpoints hardened** (orchestrator review of af8d339 items 4, 5, 6, 8 and decision O-12). They answer **`404` to everything that is not an authorised request on the admin host**: a missing or wrong key (the first version answered `401`, which confirmed the route), a request for another host, a blank key. They exist only on `Admin:Host` (host and port, case-insensitive; **required outside Development/Testing whenever a key is set, and it must not be the public host**), so they are not reachable through the public host; the web server must never proxy `/api/admin`. Refusals are counted and shown as `admin.refused` on `/health` (the key value is never logged). Their own rate-limit bucket (`RateLimits:AdminPerMinute`, 30 per client IP) so buyer and operator traffic never share a limit. The listing pages with a keyset cursor on `(created_at, id)`: `?limit=` (1 to 200, default 50) and `?cursor=` from `nextCursor`; a bad cursor is `400 invalid_cursor`; ties on the timestamp neither repeat nor skip a row. A blank or whitespace key means disabled. Tests: wrong key, prefix, key plus one character, other case, other host, host with other port, counters, buckets, paging over 9 messages with 7 tied timestamps, clamped page size. | Review items 4-6, 8; O-12. |
| M1-59 | 2026-11-04 | **Producer pattern for once-only messages.** `OutboxWriter.TryEnqueueAsync(type, payload, dedupeKey, delay)` queues the message inside the caller's own transaction **unless the dedupe key is taken**, and returns whether it queued: `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` on PostgreSQL, `INSERT OR IGNORE` on SQLite, so no earlier read and no race, and a duplicate is an "already done", never an exception that would roll back the business change next to it. **M3 to M5 must use it for payment events, order mails and every other message with a natural key**; plain `Enqueue` (saved by the caller's own `SaveChanges`) stays for messages without one. Tests: duplicate inside a transaction (the business row commits), delay honoured, ten parallel producers (one message, ten committed changes, on SQLite and PostgreSQL). | Review item 9. |
| M1-60 | 2026-11-04 | **Alerts on `/health`.** `outbox.deadLettered` counts messages dead-lettered since start-up (a rise means a person must look: a refused key, a payment event the portal will not take) and `admin.refused` counts refused operations requests; both are in-memory counts for a monitor to alert on, not a history (the admin listing is the history). | Review item 3. |
| M1-61 | 2026-11-04 | **Audit of the tests rewritten in 02c0e6a (review 4), old to new and why** (orchestrator verification of review 4, item a). (1) `Terminal_states_are_never_reopened_by_an_older_or_newer_non_terminal_event` is split into `Terminal_states_are_never_reopened_and_cancelled_is_absorbing` and `Delivered_may_become_refunded_but_not_cancelled_and_an_older_terminal_event_is_ignored`, with `The_terminal_step_table_follows_the_portal_state_machine` and `Other_modules_add_their_own_legal_steps`: the old test let any later terminal event apply (it accepted `refund.issued` after `order.cancelled`); decision O-10 makes terminal states absorbing except explicit pairs, so that expectation was **tightened** (cancelled to refunded is now refused). (2) `A_terminal_state_in_one_field_group_blocks_non_terminal_events_in_another` became `A_terminal_state_blocks_every_field_group_even_one_that_already_has_a_row` plus `A_terminal_event_older_than_the_current_terminal_state_is_ignored_even_from_another_field_group`: the terminal state now lives on one row per aggregate, the old test only tried a group without a row and ended by accepting `order.delivered` after `order.cancelled` (now illegal), so it was **tightened**. (3) `Events_with_equal_times_both_apply` became `Events_with_equal_times_are_ordered_by_event_id_whatever_order_they_arrive_in` and `Times_are_compared_at_the_stored_microsecond_precision_with_the_event_id_as_tie_breaker`: the **expectation changed on purpose**: two events with the same time no longer both overwrite in arrival order; the higher event id wins whichever arrives first, so the final state does not depend on delivery order (review 4, ties). (4) `Concurrent_replayers_apply_a_parked_event_once` became `Concurrent_replayers_neither_throw_nor_apply_an_event_twice_even_when_the_handler_creates_the_first_state_row` and `A_replay_that_loses_the_race_to_create_the_first_state_row_against_a_live_event_leaves_its_event_parked`: it now provokes the real PostgreSQL unique violation (two replayers creating the first state row) instead of counting markers; its claim assertion moved to `A_replay_whose_claim_is_taken_by_another_replayer_applies_nothing_and_leaves_the_event_parked` (M1-62), which replaces the vacuous `A_parked_event_is_claimed_before_it_is_applied_so_a_second_claim_gets_nothing` (that test set the status to Applied, so the claim was never reached). (5) `Retention_purges_payloads_then_rows_but_never_parked_events` became `Retention_purges_payloads_then_rows_and_a_parked_event_expires_after_30_days` plus `A_parked_event_that_has_not_yet_expired_keeps_its_payload_and_can_still_be_applied`: the **expectation changed on purpose** (M1-43): a parked event no longer lives for ever, its payload (message text, addresses) goes at 30 days. | The "no test deleted or weakened" rule must be auditable. |
| M1-62 | 2026-11-04 | **Review-4 follow-ups** (orchestrator verification of 02c0e6a, items b to e). (b) The webhook body cap was only tested through the in-process test server, which declares a Content-Length, so removing the in-read cap survived; the new test hosts the real app on Kestrel and sends events over a raw TCP socket as `Transfer-Encoding: chunked` with no Content-Length (exactly 256 KB accepted, one byte over and 2 MB are `413`); the old test stays and its comment no longer claims more than it proves. (c) The claim is now tested deterministically: a command interceptor runs another replayer's claim between our replay reading the event and claiming it (status still parked, version moved): the replay applies nothing, the handler is not run, the event stays parked, and the next pass applies it. (d) **One failing event no longer stops a replay**: any exception of one event (a database conflict or a handler that throws) rolls its transaction back, leaves the event parked and lets the rest of the list and the `LastReplayAt` stamp go on; only a cancelled caller stops the replay, and a cancellation is not logged as a failure; only the exception type is logged. A permanently throwing event stays parked until it expires (M1-43). (e) The commit is `CommitAsync(CancellationToken.None)` on both paths so a client disconnect between "saved" and "committed" cannot undo work; tests cancel the token right after the final save (an interceptor) and the event and its changes still commit. The parked expiry boundary is exact: 29 days 23:59:59 and exactly 30 days survive, 30 days and one second expire. 10 mutations verified (one more found, the cancellation-as-failure logging, and fixed with a test). | Review of 02c0e6a, items 7 to 11; rule "no test weakened". |
| M1-63 | 2026-11-04 | **One sign-in gate: progressive delay per address and a failure budget per IP** (security review of 09ea829 F1, F3, F4, F7; decision O-17; replaces the hard lock). Every password check (sign-in, verify-email, step-up re-authentication) first **claims an attempt** at `SignInGate`, atomically, before any password work; the claim counts the attempt as a failure up front and a right password refunds it, so 20 parallel attempts cannot all pass a check that was read before it was written (a compare-and-set on the failure count, retried; on contention the attempt waits). Per address (`signin_attempts`, key = keyed hash of the normalised address): the first **5** failures cost no delay; after failure n the next attempt must wait **1 s, 2 s, 4 s ... doubling, capped at 15 min** (the 6th attempt is still allowed and sets the 1 s). Known and unknown addresses are treated **identically** (a row is made for any address, so the 429, its `Retry-After` and when it comes are the same for both); a delayed attempt does no password work for either. The memory fades after 24 quiet hours. A right password and a successful password reset delete the row. Per IP (`signin_ip_budgets`, key = keyed hash of the forwarded client IP): **20 failures an hour** across all addresses (`Auth:SignInIpFailuresPerHour`), refunded by a success, not charged for an attempt the address delay refused; stored in the database so N instances share one budget instead of getting N times (the in-memory per-address limiter is gone). The keys are HMACs derived from `Auth:EmailLinkKey` for this purpose only, so the tables hold neither an address nor an IP. **Trade-off, accepted:** an attacker who keeps failing for an address holds it at the 15-minute delay (a denial of service for that person's sign-in), but each failure costs them IP budget (20 an hour per IP) and the owner always has "forgot password", which clears the address. A hard lock was worse: it was cheaper to trigger, longer, and told known addresses from unknown ones. Tests: identical answers over 10 tries for known and unknown, the exact schedule to the cap, the table, 20 parallel attempts (at most 6 checked, SQLite and PostgreSQL), per-IP budget and refund, a quiet day, reset clears. | Orchestrator security review F1, F3, F4, F7; O-17. |
| M1-64 | 2026-11-04 | **Pre-verification takeover closed** (F2, decision O-16). The attack: the victim signs up, the attacker signs up again with the same address and their own password, the victim clicks the newest mail, and the attacker can sign in. Now: (a) a sign-up for an existing **unverified** address changes nothing (password, name, relay address) and only re-sends verification (same `204`, older link dies); (b) **`POST /auth/verify-email` takes `{ token, password }`**: it needs the account's current password, a wrong password is the same generic `400 invalid_token` as a bad link and the link is not used up (and the check passes through the gate of M1-63, so the link cannot be used to guess); a missing password is `422` on `password`; (c) someone who does not know the password uses **forgot-password, which now mails any existing account, verified or not**, and a successful reset marks the address verified (proof of the mailbox). The verification mail says to enter the password chosen at sign-up or use "Forgot password". Web impact: the verify page must ask for the password. | Security review F2; O-16. |
| M1-65 | 2026-11-04 | **Forgot and resend do the same work for every address** (F5). A known address cost 3.4 ms and an unknown 1.1 ms on PostgreSQL, distributions that did not overlap. Now an address with no matching account performs the **same statements in the same order**: one UPDATE that matches nothing, one email-token row (purpose `decoy`, already used and expired, so it is no link) and one outbox row (type `auth.decoy`, written already delivered, never dispatched), then the same SaveChanges; a verified address on "resend" takes the same decoy path. Both kinds are purged within an hour. Rows an attacker can cause are limited by the per-address mail limit and the per-IP rate limit. The test records the statements (verb and tables, batching included) for a verified, an unverified and an unknown address and requires them equal, for both endpoints. Sign-up is not equalised beyond one hash of work for every path (counted by test); its remaining difference is a few rows against a 100 ms hash. | Security review F5. |

Whoever holds it can read that order (M5), so M3 mails it to the checkout address only. An account link and a guest link can never stand in for each other (different key, prefix and signed text; tested even with equal key text). **Claiming:** `IGuestOrderClaimer` is the seam for whoever stores guest orders: after an address is PROVEN (a successful verify-email, a successful password reset, or a successful sign-in of a verified account) every registered claimer is asked to attach the guest orders placed with that exact (trimmed, lower-cased) address to the account. Claimers must be idempotent and cheap (one conditional UPDATE); one that throws is logged by exception type only (the message may name the address) and skipped, it never fails the sign-in and the others still run. **Residual, for M3:** a guest can type someone else's address at checkout; those orders would appear for whoever can read that mailbox and verifies it, never for anyone else; the order's shipping address is the guest's own, so nothing of the mailbox owner's is exposed. | M1-75 | 2026-11-04 | **A session cannot outlive a reset or sign-out-all, even in a race (review M3).** On PostgreSQL READ COMMITTED a rotation (or a sign-in) that inserts its new session while a reset or sign-out-all runs `UPDATE ... WHERE revoked_at IS NULL` is not seen by that UPDATE, so the new session survived. Instead of locks, the account has a **`SessionEpoch`** (integer) that a reset and sign-out-all increase, and every session carries the epoch it was made under (read from the account when it is created, copied from the old session by a rotation). A session authenticates only while its epoch equals the account's, so a session made under the old epoch is dead on arrival whatever the interleaving; the revoking UPDATE stays for tidiness. No clock is involved. The tests plant the row the race would leave (an unrevoked session with the old epoch) and expect `401`, check that a rotation keeps the epoch and a new sign-in takes the current one, and were mutation-checked one by one. A sign-in takes the epoch from the very row whose password it checked, not from a second read (E1: a reset landing between the two reads used to give a session made with the old password the new epoch; found by the second review, tested with an interceptor that applies the reset at that moment); a rotation copies the old session's epoch (E2: the test now uses different values so copying the account's current epoch fails it). Both columns are 64-bit (E5). The password change uses the same epoch: it bumps it and re-stamps the session that asked, so that session lives on and every other one (even one being created at that moment) is dead. | Independent review M3. |

| M3-16 | 2026-11-04 | **Refund amounts and tax, corrected after the portal's answer to C9** (portal main 7c2e045; correction of M3-13, whose "add proportional tax on top" over-refunded partial refunds). A full cancel backs out all the tax not yet backed out; a refund without lines (goodwill amount, shipping only) prorates the seller order's remaining tax by the same floor rule; when the portal adds an explicit `taxCents` to `refund.issued` / `order.cancelled` (additive, promised) it is read instead of derived. (1) **Races:** every writer of a cart's lines (add, quantity change, merge) first updates the cart row (`LockAsync`: takes its row lock and stamps `UpdatedAt`; purge deletes the row, which takes the same lock). An add runs in a transaction under that lock, counts lines and inserts atomically, and when the cart has vanished (merged into a buyer's, purged) it starts over on a NEW cart (a guest gets a new token, returned once) instead of writing to it or answering 200 for a line that was not stored; a statement that stores nothing is never a 200 (4 attempts, then `503 cart_busy`). The merge takes the locks of both carts in a fixed order (guest, then buyer), reads the guest lines AFTER the claim, then deletes the guest cart: an add that raced it either finished before (and is merged) or waits and lands in a new cart (tested deterministically with a hook that holds the merge inside its locks: the add waits, then both answer 200, nothing lost, exactly one new guest cart, the old token dead; plus a repeated add/merge storm that checks 3 units in, 3 stored). The same lock makes the **50-line cap atomic** (45 + 15 parallel adds of different variants: exactly 5 fit, the rest `422 cart_full`) and keeps retention honest (every change stamps `UpdatedAt`; tested across 20/29/31-day steps). Honest limit: SQLite serialises writers anyway, so the lock's real work is on PostgreSQL, which the CI PostgreSQL job runs. Hooks for tests live in a per-application `CartHooks` singleton (a static would leak between parallel test classes). (2) **One currency:** the cart is USD (`CartLimits.Currency`); adding a listing priced otherwise is `422 currency_mismatch`; a line whose listing is not USD (also after a later change, or merged from a guest) is `unavailable` and blocks checkout; the view's currency is always USD, so a euro line can never turn the cart into euros. (4) **Lines follow the variant's CURRENT listing** (seller, brand, title, slug, availability), not the listing id stored at add time. A bug found by the "variant removed by the sync" test is fixed: the view threw for a line whose variant no longer exists (a non-nullable price read from a missing left-join row); it is now an `unavailable` line. (5) **A price change does NOT block checkout** (my earlier wording said it did; docs and test names now agree): the view shows `previousUnitPriceCents`, and checkout accepts an optional `expectedTotalCents` (the total the buyer saw): when present and different from the server's total nothing is created and the answer is `409 price_changed` with the new quote to show and confirm. The number is never charged or used to price anything (the intent is the server's total; tested). The web must always send it. (6) `Cache-Control: private, no-store` on every `/api/v1/cart` and `/api/v1/checkout` answer, also 401, 404 and 429 (middleware before the rate limiter; tested on each). | M1-80 | 2026-11-05 | **Passkeys (WebAuthn), as a sign-in method and as a second way to prove who the buyer is.** Library: **Fido2NetLib 4.3.2** (package `Fido2`, MIT licence, net10 build; chosen over writing CBOR/COSE/attestation verification ourselves; 4.0.0 was refused by the build because a transitive package carries a known vulnerability, 4.3.2 is clean). The protocol belongs to the library, the rules around it to us. **Relying party:** `Auth:WebAuthnRpId` and `Auth:WebAuthnOrigins` from configuration, required in production and validated at start-up in every environment (plain lower-case domain, no scheme, port or address; origins bare `scheme://host[:port]`, https in production, each host the RP id or below it); development and tests default to `localhost`. **Challenges** live in `webauthn_challenges`: server side, single use (consumed by one atomic UPDATE: right purpose, unused, not expired, made for this account AND this session), five minutes, purged an hour after expiry; a registration or a step-up is bound to the account and the session that asked, a sign-in to nothing but its purpose, and a challenge of one purpose never works for another. **Registration** (`POST /account/passkeys/options`, then `POST /account/passkeys { challengeId, name, credential }`) needs a sign-in within 10 minutes (O-11, so adding is as sensitive as changing the password), asks for a resident key and user verification, takes no attestation (`none`), excludes the passkeys already there, and answers `400 invalid_challenge` (unknown, used, expired or someone else's challenge), `400 invalid_credential` (verification failed: wrong origin, relying party, challenge, ceremony or no user verification; never a server error for hostile input), `409 already_registered` (any account), `409 too_many_passkeys` (10), `422` for a bad name. **Sign-in** (`POST /auth/passkey/options`, anonymous and discoverable, then `POST /auth/passkey/sign-in { challengeId, credential }`) verifies the signature, origin, relying party and user verification against the stored public key, checks the user handle (the account id as bytes: opaque, no personal data) and the signature counter, which is written back only if nobody else moved it (a replay or a clone loses), and then opens a session like the password sign-in (verified account, not deleted, the epoch of the row that was read, guest orders claimed); every failure is the same `401 invalid_credentials`. **Step-up** (`POST /auth/reauthenticate/passkey/options`, then `/auth/reauthenticate/passkey`) stamps `authenticated_at` of the session, with one of the buyer's own passkeys only. **List, rename, remove:** `GET /account/passkeys` (no key material), `PATCH /account/passkeys/{id}`, `DELETE /account/passkeys/{id}` (recent sign-in; another buyer's id is `404`; the last way to sign in cannot go: `409 last_sign_in_method`, under the account's row lock so two parallel removals cannot take both of the last two). **The owner is told** by mail when a passkey is added or removed (ids only in the outbox). **A password reset removes every passkey**: a reset is account recovery, and whatever got in (a stolen session that added a passkey) must not survive it; the owner adds theirs again. **Erasure:** passkeys and challenges go with the account. Tables `buyer_passkeys` (credential id unique, public key only, counter) and `webauthn_challenges`. Tests use a software authenticator (ES256, `none` attestation) and cover recency, every challenge rule, each way a registration or an assertion can be wrong, replay and a stale counter, the counter race, the epoch race, step-up, rename, removal and the last-method rule, mail, reset, erasure, retention and the configuration rules. Mutation checks (33): all killed except guards that are doubled on purpose and cannot be observed alone: the challenge's account binding (the session binding already implies it), the step-up's own `AccountId` filter on the passkey (the library already refuses a credential outside `allowCredentials`) and the owner filter on the final DELETE (the ownership check in the same locked transaction comes first); and the account row lock of the removal, which SQLite's serialised writers hide: the PostgreSQL run kills it (baseline 3/3 green, mutant 0/3). | Brief 1 (passkeys as sign-in and second method; W1-35). |
| M1-81 | 2026-11-05 | **Unicode normalisation in managed code (resolves the deferral in M1-68).** The application runs with `InvariantGlobalization`, where `string.Normalize()` silently does nothing, so a value typed with a combining accent and the same value typed with the precomposed letter were two different values. `Text/UnicodeNormalizer` (public, for every module: `Nfc`, `Nfd`, `IsNfc`, `CombiningClass`) implements UAX #15 forms C and D: full canonical decomposition, canonical ordering of marks, composition with the blocking rule, Hangul by arithmetic, lone surrogate halves passed through, never throws, and text that cannot change (everything below U+0300 for NFC, below U+00C0 for NFD) is returned as the same object. The data is `Text/UnicodeNormalizationData.cs` (2061 canonical decompositions, 388 combining-class ranges, 941 composition pairs), **generated** by `tools/unicode/generate_normalization.py` from Python's `unicodedata`, pinned to **Unicode 15.1.0** (the version of ICU 74 and the .NET runtime; the generator refuses to run with another version so a change is deliberate); composition exclusions are derived (a two-character decomposition whose NFC is the character itself), so there is no separate exclusion file. **Verification** (unicode.org is not reachable from the build machine, so the official NormalizationTest.txt could not be used): (1) `NormalizationTests`: every vector from an independent implementation (Python's own algorithm: every code point that changes, 2650 seeded random sequences with long runs of marks and Hangul), all 1,112,064 code points, every Hangul syllable by arithmetic, the defining properties (idempotence, NFD(NFC(x)) = NFD(x), NFC(NFD(x)) = NFC(x)) and known cases of the standard (singletons, exclusions, non-starter decompositions that GROW under NFC, blocking); (2) `tools/NormalizationCheck` (in the solution; run it where ICU exists: `dotnet run -c Release --project tools/NormalizationCheck [samples]`) compares with .NET's ICU-backed `string.Normalize()`: **no difference** over all 1,112,064 code points and **20,000,000** seeded random strings of starters, marks of every class, Hangul and composites (the runtime refuses one input, a noncharacter, which ours leaves unchanged). Run both again whenever the Unicode version of .NET changes. **Where it is used.** Emails: `NormaliseEmail` = trim, NFC, lower-case, NFC, so one address typed two ways is one account and the mail goes to the stored form; the length is measured on that form. Passwords: hashed in NFC; verified in NFC and, when the NFC does not match and differs from what was typed, as typed, because a hash made before this decision was made from the text as typed; a match that way asks for a rehash, which stores the NFC (the existing silent rehash at sign-in), and `BurnTime` makes the same number of checks as `Verify` for the same input; the minimum and maximum length and "not your email address" are counted on the NFC. Names, address lines and passkey names: trimmed and NFC at validation AND at storage, with the length checked on the stored form (a few characters grow when composed, U+0344 becomes two, and the columns have limits). For other modules: use `AuthValidation.NormaliseEmail` wherever an email is compared with an account's (M3's checkout lower-cases guest addresses on its own at `CheckoutEndpoints` and `OrdersAccountEraser`, which would miss a non-NFC address), and `UnicodeNormalizer.Nfd` plus dropping the non-spacing marks for diacritic-folded search tokens (M2). Mutation checks: 20 on the normaliser and its use, all killed (algorithm: ordering, blocking, Hangul, quick-path limits, surrogate pairs; use: each normalisation point, the raw-form fallback and its rehash, each length check). | M1-68 follow-up; the orchestrator's brief for the normaliser slice. |

No handler has to do anything, so a new handler cannot forget it. (1) At erasure `PortalEventsAccountEraser` (module `portal-inbound-events` of the PII registry, inside the redaction transaction) nulls `payload_json` of the account's rows that are not parked and sets `payload_purged_at`. (2) A PARKED event keeps its payload (it is replayed from it); when the replay finishes it for an account that is deleted, the same transaction drops the payload, and the 30-day parked expiry bounds the wait. (3) An event that arrives after the account's `DeletedAt` is set (not only after redaction) is stored without its payload, so the window between delete and redact leaks nothing. Rows stay (id guard, 90 days). The fulfilment outbox replay of a payload that is gone already ends dead as `event_gone`, which is right for a deleted buyer. `StripeEvent` holds only the event id and type (no payload): nothing to erase. Tests: `InboundEventErasureTests` (stored, applied, parked-then-finished, late arrival, delete-before-redact, guest and unknown references, booking/quote/conversation/message resolution). | M3-40 follow-up. |
| M1-83 | 2026-11-05 | **An existing database that is older than the model is made visible (orchestrator ask, "error after sign-in" on the test site).** `EnsureCreated` creates a database once and never alters it, so a database made by an earlier build lacks the tables and columns added since (`buyer_accounts.SessionEpoch`, `buyer_sessions.Epoch`, the passkey tables ...) and the first query that touches one is a 500. (1) At start (not in Testing), after the Development `EnsureCreated`, `SchemaCheck.LogAtStartup` compares the database with the model and writes ONE critical log line with the number of differences, the first 15 and the command that fixes them; it never stops the start and never throws (a database it cannot read gives a warning). It compares tables, columns (PostgreSQL: store type and nullability; SQLite: nullability only, SQLite types are affinities), and by name the primary keys, foreign keys, unique constraints (PostgreSQL) and indexes (both). (2) `tools/SchemaTool` (`dotnet run --project tools/SchemaTool -- "<connection string>" <postgres\|sqlite>`) lists the differences and prints the SQL that adds what is missing, generated by EF from the model: whole missing tables with their keys, missing columns (a NOT NULL column gets its type's default, e.g. `SessionEpoch ... NOT NULL DEFAULT 0`, so existing rows take it), missing indexes; a changed type or nullability, or a missing key on an existing table, is a `-- MANUAL:` comment for a person. It only reads: nothing is run for the operator; run the script in a transaction on a copy first. Tests (SQLite always, PostgreSQL in the PG job): a current database has no differences; dropped columns, tables and an index are listed, and running the script makes the differences empty; a row survives a NOT NULL column being added; wrong type listed as manual (PG); the start-up line is one critical line and nothing when all is well; an unreadable database only warns. The InitialCreate slice keeps this check and turns it into the refusal for a database without history (M1-84 there). **Review lows (orchestrator):** (1) `SchemaCheck.OpenReadOnly` is how the tool reads: SQLite with `Mode=ReadOnly` (a mistyped path fails, no empty database is created; tested), PostgreSQL with `default_transaction_read_only=on` for the session (a CREATE is refused; tested). (2) The start-up warning keeps the reason, passed through `SchemaCheck.Sanitise`: connection-string pairs (Password, Pwd, User ID, Host, Server, Data Source, Database, Port ...) become `key=***`, one line, at most 300 characters, so "connection refused" and "permission denied" can be told apart; tested with a password and a data-source path that must not appear. (3) `tools/SchemaTool` takes the provider as its argument and the connection string from `MARKETPLACE_DB` or from standard input (`-`), never from the command line (shell history, process list); a connection string on the command line is refused with the usage text; its failures print the sanitised reason and exit 3. (4) PostgreSQL-only tests keep returning early without PostgreSQL, and the README now says so (a plain local `dotnet test` passes them without having run them; CI's PostgreSQL job runs them). Reporting them as skipped, and excluding them from the SQLite job with a trait filter so the merge commit shows 0 skipped, was considered and NOT done: it needs an edit of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` that changes which tests a CI job selects, and that is the repository owner's decision (the standing rule is that no test is skipped, deleted or weakened), not a worker's. | Orchestrator ask after M1-82; README, Database schema. |

(LOW-1) Registration counts the cap under the account row lock in the transaction that inserts (the earlier count stays as the cheap early answer); test: a completion that read "one of two" while another took the second place is refused (hook between its count and its transaction); the mutant without the count in the transaction is killed. (LOW-2) `Recent(who, Now)` is checked again at completion (before the challenge is consumed, so the same challenge works after a step-up); test at 9 + 4 minutes. (LOW-3, my call) A password change and sign-out-everywhere KEEP passkeys (a passkey is a way in of its own, and removing them silently could lock out someone who relies on them), but: the password-changed mail now says so and how to remove an unknown one; `POST /account/password` takes `removePasskeys: true` (same transaction; the account has a password, so a way in remains); `DELETE /account/passkeys` removes all (recent sign-in; refused as last_sign_in_method without a password). The password RESET already removes all of them (M1-80). The web should offer the box and the button. (LOW-4) A discoverable sign-in refuses a response without a user handle and one whose handle is not the credential owner's; for step-up, where the credential list names the account, the handle stays optional (the library's own owner check still runs; the equality check in SignInAsync is a redundant guard behind it: its mutant survives and is recorded). (LOW-6) The 16 KB auth body cap also covers `/api/v1/account/passkeys` (413 `too_large`). (LOW-8) In production the RP id must not be a public suffix: a built-in list of common multi-label suffixes (`co.uk`, `github.io`, `herokuapp.com`, `vercel.app` ...; `StartupValidator.PublicSuffixes`, not the whole Public Suffix List) is refused with an explanation; `Testing` outside tests is NOT refused: it behaves like Development, which is already allowed anywhere, so it adds no exposure. (Unverified item) NuGet shows libsodium up to 1.0.22 and NSec.Cryptography up to 26.4.0; the project now pins NSec 26.4.0 (it asks for the newer libsodium) over Fido2 4.3.2's 25.4.0, clean build and the 64 passkey tests green on SQLite and PostgreSQL; libsodium is reached only by EdDSA (Ed25519) passkeys (the library's default parameters allow them; ES256 and RS256 use the platform's crypto), and I could not read the advisory text from here, so whether 1.0.20.1 is actually affected is unconfirmed. | Orchestrator security review of c0fb1bc. |
| M1-86 | 2026-11-05 | **A flaky address-book test, and a 500 that should be a 503 (orchestrator, CI run on 2143e48).** `A_storm_of_mixed_address_writes_always_ends_with_exactly_one_default_when_any_address_is_left` failed once in CI on SQLite (one of 30 concurrent writes answered 500, "The address book is too busy" after its fixed retries). I could not reproduce it in 192 locally stressed runs (one failure in 36 under heavy parallel load, whose exception I could not capture), and reading the code shows no way for a unique violation to survive the account row lock, so the cause is unproven; the fix therefore makes the behaviour right whatever the cause. (1) The address writes (create, update, set default, delete) retry on `AccountService.IsRetryable`: a unique violation as before, plus SQLite busy/locked (5, 6) and PostgreSQL serialization failure and deadlock (40001, 40P01); anything else is a real error and is not retried. (2) Between attempts there is a short random back-off that grows with the attempt (2 to 11 ms times the attempt number), so requests that collided do not collide again in step, and the attempts rise from 8 to 12. (3) When the attempts run out the service throws `TooBusyException`, which `ProblemExceptionMiddleware` answers with **503 `too_busy` and `Retry-After: 1`**: a retryable answer, not a 500. (4) The storm test accepts 503 (with Retry-After) as an outcome of a write under contention but keeps every other assertion, in particular exactly one default whenever any address is left, unchanged. New tests inject SQLite busy, locked and constraint errors into the writes: a write that meets four conflicts is retried and succeeds (and ends with one default); one that never stops meeting them is a 503 with Retry-After for all four operations and leaves the address book as it was; `IsRetryable` accepts only the lock and unique conflicts. Mutants (retry codes removed, each of the four exhaustion sites back to a plain exception, Retry-After removed) are killed. If the 500 ever shows again in CI the log line carries the exception type, so it can be traced. | Orchestrator flake report. |
| M1-87 | 2026-11-05 | **Session rotation on a password change and on a passkey removal (web review MED-3, orchestrator).** `POST /account/password` used to revoke only the OTHER sessions and keep the caller's token valid, so someone who changes the password because they think their cookie was stolen did not kill the thief's copy of THAT cookie. Now it ends EVERY session of the account, the caller's too (the epoch moves up and all rows are revoked in the same transaction), opens a fresh one at the new epoch and returns `200 { token }` (before: `204`); the old token answers 401 afterwards. Same for `DELETE /account/passkeys/{id}` and `DELETE /account/passkeys` (`AuthService.RotateSessionsAsync`, inside the removal's transaction under the account row lock): removing a passkey you do not recognise must also end the session it may have been added from; they answer `200 { token }` instead of `204`. A refused request (403, 404, 409, wrong password) changes nothing and rotates nothing. **Contract change for the web (M2):** replace the stored session token with the one returned by these three calls (the cookie rotates). Tests: the old token is 401 and the new one works after a password change (also with a session created during the change, the epoch above 32 bits and a session that moved while the check ran), after a single removal and after remove-all; the parallel-removal test signs in per round and accepts 409 or 401 for the loser (when the winner's rotation already ended its token) but still demands exactly one success and one passkey left. | Web review MED-3. |

Now `DatabaseSetup.RefuseUnknownHistory` runs first: any applied migration id the build does not have stops the start with a critical log and an exception naming the ids and the fix, and changes nothing; it also covers a rollback to an older build after a newer one migrated (the message says so). The fix is one line, in the README (Database schema, migrations): `DELETE FROM "__EFMigrationsHistory";` and restart; the start then replays the migrations, compares, and baselines the database or lists what differs (for the older InitialCreate that is `buyer_accounts.DemoVerifiedAt`, which `tools/SchemaTool` adds). Tests (PostgreSQL): the stranger refuses with the ids, the fix text and no "already exists", history and tables untouched; the documented fix baselines an equal database; the whole path for the older InitialCreate (refuse, clear the history, refuse with the missing column, run the tool's SQL, baselined). (2) The storm test, on an unexpected status, now fails with the server log lines (Unhandled type and stack, Too busy with the conflict types: never messages) in the assertion text; the M1-86 logging already covers it, so the next flake names its exception (CI run 41994503856 was a PUT answering 500; the cause is still unproven, see M1-86). | Orchestrator M9 review notes. |

(MED-2) The history table and ALL history rows are written in ONE transaction under the advisory lock (test seam `DatabaseSetup.HistoryRowWritten`: a crash after the first row leaves neither table nor row; mutant: commit before the crash killed). (MED-3) `SchemaDescription.Choose` picks the OLDEST migration whose replayed result equals the database, not the newest: a later migration that changes nothing the description sees (data, a grant) would otherwise be marked applied and never run; the ones after the match are applied by Migrate(), and a migration with no schema change is harmless to apply. `Describe` now also covers views, triggers, sequences, enum types and table comments (grants and data stay outside it). (LOW-3) `IsUniqueViolation` for SQLite now needs the extended codes 2067 (unique) or 1555 (primary key): code 19 alone covers every constraint (not null, check, foreign key), and those must not be retried. (LOW-5) The baseline waits for the advisory lock with `pg_try_advisory_lock` until a deadline (2 minutes, `LockWait`) and then stops with a `TimeoutException` that says nothing was changed; a failing unlock (a broken connection) is logged by type and never replaces the error already on its way out. Test: a held lock gives the timeout and changes nothing, then the start works once it is free. | Orchestrator review of 1d4cc33..e10ff79. |
| M5-21 | 2026-11-04 | **O-21 in full (orchestrator wording; extends M5-20).** Transactional buyer emails cannot be switched off: order confirmation, payment failure, cancellation (incl. a refused cancellation), every refund, a failed delivery attempt, every change to a return (approved, declined, received, label ready), and booking confirmed, declined, new time proposed, rescheduled, cancelled, extra charge asked, no-show; security and account emails. Optional (and the only things the `all` link and the settings page can turn off): `order_updates` (seller accepted), `shipped`, `out_for_delivery`, `delivered`, `messages`, `bookings` (reminder and completed). `GET preferences` returns `alwaysOn: [refunds, cancellations, delivery_problems, returns, booking_changes]`; a PUT that names one of those with `false` is a 422 (nothing changes), with `true` a no-op. The refund notification carries the order's `currency`.
